Adaptive Change Point Inference for High Dimensional Time Series with Temporal Dependence
Xiaoyi Wang, Jixuan Liu, Long Feng, Bejing Normal University, Nankai University

Abstract
This paper investigates change point inference in high-dimensional time series. We begin by introducing a max- L 2 L_{2} -norm based test procedure, which demonstrates strong performance under dense alternatives. We then establish the asymptotic independence between our proposed statistic and the two max- L ∞ L_{\infty} -based statistics introduced by Wang and Feng 2023 . Building on this result, we develop an adaptive inference approach by applying the Cauchy combination method to integrate these tests. This combined procedure exhibits robust performance across varying levels of sparsity. Extensive simulation studies and real data analysis further confirm the superior effectiveness of our proposed methods in the high-dimensional setting. Keywords : Cauchy combination test; Change point inference; High dimensional data; Time series.



1
Introduction

Change point inference plays a critical role in the analysis of high-dimensional data, where the structure or behavior of a system may shift over time due to external shocks, policy changes, or underlying regime switches. In modern applications—such as genomics, finance, neuroimaging, and environmental monitoring—data are often collected across a large number of variables simultaneously, making traditional low-dimensional change point detection methods inadequate. High-dimensional change point inference enables the detection and localization of structural breaks in complex systems, allowing for timely responses, better understanding of dynamic processes, and more accurate predictive modeling
(
Aston and Kirch 2018
)
. Given the growing prevalence of high-dimensional data in scientific and industrial contexts, developing robust and adaptive inference procedures that can handle varying degrees of sparsity and dependence is both practically important and theoretically challenging.

Consider a sequence of
p
-dimensional random vectors of sample size
n
,
\{\boldsymbol{X}_{i}=(X_{i1},\ldots,

X_{ip})^{\!\top\!}\}_{i=1}^{n}
, from the mean-change model,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{X}_{i}={\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{0}+{\boldsymbol{\delta}}\mathbb{I}(i>\tau)+\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{i},~~~~\ i=1,\ldots,n,

where
{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
is the baseline mean level,
{\boldsymbol{\delta}}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
is the mean-change signal parameter,

\tau\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
is the possible changepoint, and
\{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{i}=(\epsilon_{i1},\ldots,\epsilon_{ip})^{\!\top\!}\}_{i=1}^{n}
are random noises with zero mean.
Of interest is to test whether there exists a change point, that is,

\displaystyle\begin{gathered}H_{0}:\tau=n\ \text{and}\ {\boldsymbol{\delta}}=0\ \text{versus}\\
H_{1}:\ \text{there exists}\ \tau\in\{1,\ldots,n-1\}\ \text{and}\ {\boldsymbol{\delta}}\neq 0,\end{gathered}

under the paradigm that both the sample size
n
and dimension
p
grow to infinity.

Many existing studies focus on constructing test statistics by taking the maximum or the sum over the sample size or across dimensions of the corresponding individual CUSUM-type statistics. Specially, the individual cumulative sum (CUSUM) statistics
\{C_{\gamma,j}(k),\ j=1,\ldots,p\}_{k=1}^{n-1}
, with
\gamma=0
or
0.5
frequently used, is defined as

\displaystyle C_{\gamma,j}(k)=\left\{\frac{k}{n}\left(1-\frac{k}{n}\right)\right\}^{-\gamma}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\left(S_{kj}-\frac{k}{n}S_{nj}\right)/\widehat{\sigma}_{j},

(1)

S_{kj}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}X_{ij}
and
\widehat{\sigma}_{j}
’s are estimators of component-wise (long-run) variances. Among them,
Bai 2010
,
Horváth and
Hušková 2012
, and
Jin et al. 2016
proposed test statistics of the form
\max_{k=1,\ldots,n-1}\sum_{j=1}^{p}C_{0,j}^{2}(k)
, where the maximum is taken over time points and the summation is taken across dimensions. We refer to such statistics as max-
L_{2}
-type test statistics.
Wang et al. 2018
and
Wang et al. 2019
introduced sum-
L_{2}
-type statistics of the form
\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}\sum_{j=1}^{p}C_{0.5,j}^{2}(k)
, where the summation is taken over both time points and dimensions. These statistics are generally powerful under dense alternatives, in which many components of the mean-change vector
\boldsymbol{\delta}
are nonzero but of small magnitude. However, their power diminishes under sparse alternatives, where only a few entries of
\boldsymbol{\delta}
are nonzero but have large magnitudes. To address this issue, several works have proposed statistics based on maxima over the dimension. For example,
Jirak 2015
proposed a max-
L_{\infty}
-type test statistic
\max_{k=1,\ldots,n-1}\max_{j=1,\ldots,p}|C_{0,j}(k)|
with
\gamma=0
in (
1
), and demonstrated that, under
H_{0}
and suitable normalization, this statistic converges to a Gumbel-type extreme value distribution. Furthermore,
Yu and Chen 2021
considered a similar max-
L_{\infty}
-type statistic, defined as
\max_{\lambda\leq k\leq n-\lambda}\max_{j=1,\ldots,p}|C_{0.5,j}(k)|
, where
\lambda\in[1,n/2]
is a user-specified trimming parameter and
\gamma=0.5
in (
1
). Recently,
Wang and Feng 2023
established the asymptotic null distributions of both max-
L_{\infty}
-type test statistics under weaker and more general conditions.

In practical scenarios, the sparsity structure of the alternative hypothesis—whether dense or sparse—is typically unknown. To address this challenge, a number of studies have proposed adaptive testing procedures that are capable of maintaining good power across varying sparsity levels. For example,
Liu et al. 2020
introduced an adaptive strategy based on adjusted max-
L_{q}
aggregation. This method computes the test statistics for a set of values
q\in\{1,2,3,4,5,\infty\}
and then selects the minimum
p
-value among them. This approach leverages the fact that larger
q
values tend to be more powerful for sparse alternatives, whereas smaller
q
values are better suited for dense alternatives. Similarly,
Zhang et al. 2022
proposed a family of self-normalized U-statistics corresponding to each
L_{q}
norm, providing a unified framework for adaptivity. More recently,
Wang and Feng 2023
established the asymptotic independence between the max-
L_{\infty}
-type statistics proposed in
Jirak 2015
;
Yu and Chen 2021
and the sum-
L_{2}
-type statistic in
Wang et al. 2019
. Building on this result, they developed a computationally efficient Fisher-type combination test that effectively adapts to different sparsity regimes. This combined procedure demonstrates strong performance across a wide range of alternatives. For a comprehensive review of recent advances in high-dimensional change-point inference, including these adaptive strategies, see
Liu et al. 2022
,
Wang and Feng 2023
,
Meng et al. 2024
.

Most of the aforementioned methods assume that the observed time series
\boldsymbol{X}_{i}
are independent. However, in practice, high-dimensional time series often exhibit some weak temporal dependence. In such cases, many of these methods fail to work effectively and may suffer from large size distortions. To address this issue, some studies have focused on high-dimensional change point inference in the presence of temporal dependence. For example,
Li et al. 2019
proposed a sum-
L_{2}
-type test procedure that aggregates a bias-corrected version of the classic CUSUM test statistic. Meanwhile,
Wang et al. 2022
developed a trimmed version of the self-normalized U-statistic, which excludes pairs of points that are close in time. Both of these methods are sum-type test statistics over dimensions and perform well for dense alternatives. However, for sparse alternatives, it is worth noting that the two max-
L_{\infty}
-type test statistics
(
Jirak 2015
;
Yu and Chen 2021
)
can also be applied to high-dimensional temporal dependence data, provided that the variance estimator is replaced by a long-run variance estimator. To the best of our knowledge, there currently does not exist an adaptive test procedure that can handle a wide range of sparsity levels for alternatives. Therefore, in this paper, we introduce an adaptive change point inference method for high-dimensional time series with temporal dependence.

First, we propose a max-
L_{2}
-type test statistic along with a bias correction procedure to mitigate the effects of temporal dependence. We establish the limiting null distribution of the proposed test statistic. Furthermore, we demonstrate that the max-
L_{2}
-type test statistic is asymptotically independent of two existing max-
L_{\infty}
-type test statistics
(
Jirak 2015
;
Yu and Chen 2021
)
. Based on this result, we develop two Cauchy combination test procedures that combine the max-
L_{2}
-type statistic with the max-
L_{\infty}
-type statistics. Unlike the asymptotic independence between the Gumbel and normal distributions shown in
Wang and Feng 2023
, our work establishes the asymptotic independence between a Gumbel distribution and the maximum of a Gaussian process—representing a non-trivial theoretical contribution. Finally, we propose an estimator for the change-point location based on the adaptive Cauchy combination
p
-values and show its consistency under mild regularity conditions. Simulation studies and real data applications demonstrate that the proposed methods perform well in high-dimensional time series settings with temporal dependence. The Cauchy combination tests are particularly effective across a wide range of sparsity levels in the alternatives. This paper makes three key contributions:

1.

Development of a max-
L_{2}
-type test statistic: We propose a novel max-
L_{2}
-type test statistic for change-point inference in high-dimensional time series data with temporal dependence. Compared to the traditional sum-
L_{2}
-type test statistic, our proposed method exhibits significantly improved power, particularly under dense alternative scenarios.

2.

New theoretical framework for asymptotic independence: We introduce a new analytical approach to establish the asymptotic independence between a Gumbel distribution and the maximum of a Gaussian process. This result is distinct from existing techniques, which primarily focus on proving the asymptotic independence between normal and Gumbel distributions. Our contribution broadens the theoretical understanding of extreme value theory in dependent high-dimensional settings.

3.

Robust Cauchy combination testing procedure: Based on the established asymptotic independence, we construct two Cauchy combination test procedures that integrate the max-
L_{2}
-type and max-
L_{\infty}
-type test statistics. These procedures demonstrate strong and robust performance across a wide range of sparsity levels in the alternatives, making them highly suitable for practical applications involving high-dimensional, temporally dependent data.

The remainder of the paper is organized as follows. Section
2
introduces the proposed max-
L_{2}
-type test procedure. Section
3
establishes the asymptotic independence between the max-
L_{2}
-type and max-
L_{\infty}
-type test statistics and proposes two Cauchy combination test procedures. Section
4
focuses on the estimation of the change-point location. Section
5
presents simulation studies, and Section
6
provides a real data application. All technical proofs are given in the Appendix.

Throughout this paper, we use
\gtrsim,\lesssim,(\sim)
to denote (two sided) inequalities involving a multiplicative constant. For
a\in\mathbb{R}
,
\lfloor a\rfloor
and
\lceil a\rceil
denote the lower and upper integer part of
a
. For
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
, we write
a\wedge b=\mathrm{min}(a,b)
. For a vector
\mathbf{a}=(a_{1},\dots,a_{p})^{{\!\top\!}}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
,
\|\mathbf{a}\|^{2}=\sum\nolimits_{j=1}^{p}a_{j}^{2}
. For a matrix
\mathbf{A}
,
\lambda_{\mathrm{min}}(\mathbf{A})
and
\lambda_{\max}(\mathbf{A})
denote the minimum and maximum eigenvalue of matrix
\mathbf{A},
and
\mathrm{tr}(\mathbf{A})
denotes the trace of
\mathbf{A}
. For a set
\mathcal{A}
, we denote
|\mathcal{A}|
by its cardinality, and by
\mathcal{A}^{c}
its complementary.
\mathbb{I}(h=0)
denotes the indicator function.

2
max-
L_{2}
-type test

Before giving the max-
L_{2}
-type test statistic, for any
k\in\{1,\dots,n\}
, we propose the CUSUM statistic

\displaystyle W(k)=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n\sqrt{p}}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}\boldsymbol{X}_{i}-\frac{k}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\boldsymbol{X}_{i}\right)^{\top}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}\boldsymbol{X}_{i}-\frac{k}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\boldsymbol{X}_{i}\right).

It can be showed that the expectation of
W(k)
under
H_{0}
as follows,

\displaystyle\mu_{k}=\frac{k^{2}(n-k)^{2}}{n^{3}\sqrt{p}}\sum\limits_{h=0}^{n-1}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n-h}\{2-\mathbb{I}(h=0)\}a_{i,k}a_{i+h,k}\mathrm{tr}\{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}(h)\},

where

\displaystyle a_{i,k}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}k^{-1},&i\leq k,\\
-(n-k)^{-1},&i>k.\end{array}\right.

We use

\displaystyle\mu_{M,k}=\frac{k^{2}(n-k)^{2}}{n^{3}\sqrt{p}}\sum\limits_{h=0}^{M}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n-h}{\{2-\mathbb{I}(h=0)\}}a_{i,k}a_{i+h,k}\mathrm{tr}\{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}(h)\},

replace
\mu_{k}
, where
M=\lceil(n\wedge p)^{1/8}\rceil
. We have proved that the difference between
\mu_{k}
and
\mu_{M,k}
is negligible under the following Assumptions
1
–
2
in the Appendix.
In order to show the asymptotic distribution of
W(k)
, we impose the following assumptions.

A
SSUMPTION
1

Suppose the noises
\{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
follow the linear structure

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{i}={\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{1/2}\sum\limits_{\ell=0}^{\infty}b_{\ell}\boldsymbol{Z}_{i-\ell}.

(i)

\boldsymbol{Z}_{i}=(Z_{i1},\ldots,Z_{ip})^{\!\top\!}
, and
Z_{ij}
are independent and identically distributed random variables with
\mathbb{E}(Z_{ij})=0,\mathbb{E}(Z_{ij}^{2})=1,\mathbb{E}(Z_{ij}^{4})<+\infty
.

(ii)

\sum\nolimits_{\ell=0}^{\infty}|b_{\ell}|<\infty
,
\sum\nolimits_{\ell=0}^{\infty}b_{\ell}=s\neq 0
, and
\lim\limits_{\ell\rightarrow\infty}\ell^{5}b_{\ell}=0
.

A
SSUMPTION
2

There exist constants
C_{0}
and
C_{1}
such that
0\leq C_{0}<\lambda_{\mathrm{min}}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}})\leq\lambda_{\max}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}})\leq C_{1}<\infty
.

Assumption
1
assumes the linear process model of
\{\boldsymbol{X}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
, which is widely used in time series analysis
(
Zhang et al. 2018
)
. This linear process model cover the well known MA(
q
) model and AR(
1
) model. Let
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}(h)=\mathrm{cov}(\boldsymbol{X}_{i},\boldsymbol{X}_{i+h})
for
h=0,1,2,\ldots,n-1
, and
\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{n}=n\mathrm{cov}(\bar{\boldsymbol{X}}_{n})=\boldsymbol{\Gamma}(0)+2\sum\nolimits_{h=1}^{n-1}(1-n^{-1}h)\boldsymbol{\Gamma}(h)
. With Assumption
1
,
\mathbb{E}(\boldsymbol{X}_{i})={\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{0}+{\boldsymbol{\delta}}\mathbb{I}(i>\tau)
and
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}(h)=c_{h}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}
for
h=0,1,2,\dots
and
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}(h)=\boldsymbol{\Gamma}(-h)^{\top}
with
c_{h}=\sum\nolimits_{\ell=0}^{\infty}b_{\ell}b_{\ell+h}
, and the long-run covariance matrix
\boldsymbol{\Omega}=\boldsymbol{\Gamma}(0)+2\sum\nolimits_{h=1}^{\infty}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}(h)
.
Assumption
1
-(ii) guarantees that the dependence of the current error on past shocks decays. It rules out processes with unit roots or long memory where correlations decay too slowly. Assumption
2
ensures that
\mathrm{tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}})=O(p)
and
\mathrm{tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2})=O(p)
, which primarily guarantees the asymptotic independence of the test statistics. A similar assumption also appears in
Wang and Feng 2023
.

T
HEOREM
1

Under Assumptions
1
–
2
and
H_{0}
, if
M=\lceil(n\wedge p)^{1/8}\rceil
, we have

W(\lfloor nt\rfloor)-\mu_{M,\lfloor nt\rfloor}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\rightarrow}}\omega V(t),\quad t\in[0,1],

as
(n,p)\rightarrow\infty
, where
\omega=\lim_{p\rightarrow\infty}\sqrt{2\mathrm{tr}(\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{2})/p}
.

V(t)
is a continuous Gaussian process with
\mathbb{E}\{V(t)\}=0
and

\mathbb{E}\{V(t)V(s)\}=(1-t)^{2}s^{2},\quad 0\leqslant s\leqslant t\leqslant 1.

In practice, it is essential to estimate
\mu_{M,\lfloor nt\rfloor}
and
\omega
in Theorem 1. Define
\breve{\boldsymbol{X}}_{f,h,g,k}=(\boldsymbol{X}_{f}-\boldsymbol{X}_{f+M+h+1})^{{\!\top\!}}(\boldsymbol{X}_{g}-\boldsymbol{X}_{g+M+k+1})
.
Inspired by the procedure of moving ranges of neighboring samples proposed in
Wang et al. 2019
, we propose a consistent estimator of
\mu_{M,\lfloor nt\rfloor}
as follows,

\displaystyle\hat{\mu}_{M,\lfloor nt\rfloor}=\frac{\lfloor nt\rfloor^{2}(n-\lfloor nt\rfloor)^{2}}{n^{3}\sqrt{p}}\sum\limits_{h=0}^{M}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n-h}{\{2-\mathbb{I}(h=0)\}}a_{i,\lfloor nt\rfloor}a_{i+h,\lfloor nt\rfloor}\widehat{\mathrm{tr}\{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}(h)\}},

(3)

where

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathrm{tr}\{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}(h)\}}=\frac{1}{2n}\sum\limits_{t=1}^{n-M-2h-1}\breve{\boldsymbol{X}}_{t+h,h,t,h}.

Motivated by
Zhang et al. 2018
, we consider the following estimator for
\mathrm{tr}\{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}(h)\boldsymbol{\Gamma}(k)\}
,

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathrm{tr}\{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}(h)\boldsymbol{\Gamma}(k)\}}=\frac{\sum\limits_{t=1}^{[n/2]-M-2k-1}\sum\limits_{s=t+[n/2]}^{n-M-2k-1}\breve{\boldsymbol{X}}_{t,h,s,k}\breve{\boldsymbol{X}}_{t+h,h,s+k,k}}{4(n-k-3/2[n/2]-M/2)([n/2]-M-2k-1)}.

Then, we propose the ratio-consistent estimator of
\omega
,

\displaystyle\hat{\omega}=\bigg\{\frac{2}{p}\bigg(\widehat{\mathrm{tr}\{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}(0)\boldsymbol{\Gamma}(0)\}}+2\sum\limits_{h=1}^{M}\widehat{\mathrm{tr}\{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}(h)\boldsymbol{\Gamma}(0)\}}+2\sum\limits_{k=1}^{M}\widehat{\mathrm{tr}\{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}(0)\boldsymbol{\Gamma}(h)\}}+4\sum\limits_{h,k=1}^{M}\widehat{\mathrm{tr}\{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}(h)\boldsymbol{\Gamma}(k)\}}\bigg)\bigg\}^{1/2}.

(4)

The following Theorem
2
shows the consistency of the estimators.

T
HEOREM
2

Under Assumptions
1
–
2
, if
M=\lceil(n\wedge p)^{1/8}\rceil
,
p=o(n^{7/4})
and
\|{\boldsymbol{\delta}}\|^{2}=o(np^{1/2}/M^{2})
, we have

\hat{\mu}_{M,\lfloor nt\rfloor}-\mu_{M,\lfloor nt\rfloor}=o_{p}(\omega)
,
for any
t\in[0,1]
, and

\hat{\omega}/\omega\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\rightarrow}}1
, as
(n,p)\rightarrow\infty
.

Based on Theorem
1
–
2
, we finally propose the max-
L_{2}
-type test statistic,

\displaystyle S_{n,p}=\max_{t\in[0,1]}\left\{W(\lfloor nt\rfloor)-\hat{\mu}_{M,\lfloor nt\rfloor}\right\},

and the following Theorem
3
shows that the max-
L_{2}
-type test statistic
S_{n,p}
converges to a Gaussian process.

T
HEOREM
3

Under Assumptions
1
–
2
and
H_{0}
, if
M=\lceil(n\wedge p)^{1/8}\rceil
and
p=o(n^{7/4})
, we have

\displaystyle S_{n,p}/\hat{\omega}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\rightarrow}}\max_{t\in[0,1]}V(t),

as
(n,p)\rightarrow\infty
, where
V(t)
is a continuous Gaussian process with
\mathbb{E}\{V(t)\}=0
and

\mathbb{E}\{V(t)V(s)\}=(1-t)^{2}s^{2},\quad 0\leqslant s\leqslant t\leqslant 1.

The
p
-value of the test based on
S_{n,p}
is given by

\displaystyle{p}_{S_{n,p}}=1-F_{V}\left(S_{n,p}/\hat{\omega}\right),

where
F_{V}(\cdot)
is the cumulative distribution function (CDF) of
\max_{0\leq t\leq 1}V(t)
. If the
p
-value is smaller than the pre-specified significance level
\alpha\in(0,1)
, we reject the null hypothesis, that is, there are no changepoints in the data sequence.

R
EMARK
2.1

Following Equation (15) in
Konakov et al. 2020
, there exist a constant
c
, such that

\displaystyle\mathrm{P}\left(\max_{t\in[0,1]}V(t)\geq u\right)=\frac{c}{u}e^{-8u^{2}}\{1+o(1)\}.

The constant
c
could be estimated via Monte Carlo simulation. Specifically, let
T=\{t_{i}=i/T_{d},i=1,\dots,T_{d}\}
and the number of replications
B
. For
b=1,\dots,B
, let
v_{b}=\max_{t\in T}V_{b}(t)
, where
\left(V_{b}(1),\dots,V_{b}(T_{d})\right)^{{\!\top\!}}
is sample from the
T_{d}
-dimensional multivariate normal distribution with mean zero and covariance matrix with the
(k,\ell)
th element given by
(1-t_{k})^{2}t_{\ell}^{2}
for
1\leq k\leq\ell\leq T_{d}
. Naturally, the estimated CDF of
\max_{0\leq t\leq 1}V(t)
can be constructed with
\{v_{b}\}_{b=1}^{B}
, and the estimation
\hat{c}
will be obtained with a given nominal significant level
\alpha
.
Thus, the
p
-value can be alternatively calculated as

\displaystyle p_{S_{n,p}}=\frac{\hat{\omega}\hat{c}}{S_{n,p}}e^{-8S_{n,p}^{2}/\hat{\omega}^{2}}.

Similar to the approach of
Jin et al. 2016
, the test based on
S_{n,p}
can be expected that
\max
-
L_{2}
-type test performs well in detecting small but dense signals. The following proposition established the test consistency under
H_{1}
.

P
ROPOSITION
2.1

Under the assumptions in Theorem
2
and
H_{1}
, if
\tau\sim n
and
\|{\boldsymbol{\delta}}\|^{2}\gtrsim p^{1/2}n^{-1}
, then we have, the test based on
S_{n,p}
is consistent, as
(n,p)\rightarrow\infty
.

3
Adaptive tests

We first restate two versions of
\max
-
L_{\infty}
-type statistics that were adopted in the literature, to wit,

\displaystyle M_{n,p}:=\max_{k=1,\ldots,n-1}\max_{j=1,\ldots,p}|C_{0,j}(k)|\ \text{and}\ M^{\dagger}_{n,p}:=\max_{\lambda_{n}\leq k\leq n-\lambda_{n}}\max_{j=1,\ldots,p}|C_{0.5,j}(k)|,

respectively, where we recall that the CUSUM statistics
C_{\gamma,j}(k)
’s with
\gamma=0
or 0.5 are defined in (
1
), and
\lambda_{n}\in[1,n/2]
is a pre-specified boundary removal parameter.
We also restate Theorem 1 in
Wang and Feng 2023
with some mild modifications.

T
HEOREM
4

Suppose
H_{0}
and Assumptions 1–2 in
Wang and Feng 2023
hold and the component-wise (long-run) estimators
\widehat{\sigma}_{j}
in (
1
) satisfy the uniform convergence
\max_{1\leq j\leq p}|\widehat{\sigma}_{j}-\sigma_{j}|=o(1)
. If
p\lesssim n^{\nu}
for some
0<\nu<q/2-2
, we have

(i)

As
(n,p)\to\infty
,

\mathrm{P}\left(M_{n,p}\leq u_{p}\{\exp(-x)\}\right)\to\exp\{-\exp(-x)\},

where
u_{p}\{\exp(-x)\}=\sqrt{\{x+\mathrm{log}(2p)\}/2}
.

(ii)

If
\lambda_{n}\sim n^{\lambda}
for some
\lambda\in(0,1)
, then, as
(n,p)\to\infty
,

\mathrm{P}\left(M^{\dagger}_{n,p}\leq\frac{x+D(p\mathrm{log}h_{n})}{A(p\mathrm{log}h_{n})}\right)\to\exp\{-\exp(-x)\},

where
A(x)=\sqrt{2\mathrm{log}x}
,
D(x)=2\mathrm{log}x+2^{-1}\mathrm{log}\mathrm{log}x-2^{-1}\mathrm{log}\pi
and
h_{n}=\left\{(\lambda_{n}/n)^{-1}-1\right\}^{2}
.

For the variance estimator
\hat{\sigma}_{j}
,
j\in\{1,\ldots,p\}
, we follow the difference-based approach proposed by
Chan 2022
, which may provides a more efficient variance estimation method under change-point scenarios
(
Chan 2022
)
. Specifically, we employ the third-order difference-based long-run variance estimator applied componentwise, with the bandwidth optimally selected to minimize the mean squared error; see
\hat{v}_{(3)}
and Equation (6.3) in
Chan 2022
. If we further assume that
\mathbb{E}(Z_{ij}^{4+r})<\infty
for some
r>0
, where
Z_{ij}
is defined in Assumption
1
for
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
and
j\in\{1,\ldots,p\}
, or alternatively impose Assumption
3
directly, under additional assumptions on the parameters and kernel function used in the statistic, the desired consistency hold. For details, see the Lemma
8.10
in Appendix.

By Theorem
4
, the
p
-values associated with
M_{n,p}
and
M^{\dagger}_{n,p}
are

\displaystyle p_{M_{n,p}}

\displaystyle=1-G\big(2M_{n,p}^{2}-\mathrm{log}(2p)\big),

\displaystyle p_{M^{\dagger}_{n,p}}

\displaystyle=1-G\big(A(p\,\mathrm{log}\,\lambda^{\dagger}_{n})M^{\dagger}_{n,p}-D(p\,\mathrm{log}\,\lambda^{\dagger}_{n})\big),

where
\lambda^{\dagger}_{n}=\{(\lambda_{n}/n)^{-1}-1\}^{2}
,
A(x)=\sqrt{2\,\mathrm{log}\,x}
,
D(x)=2\,\mathrm{log}\,x+2^{-1}\mathrm{log}\,\mathrm{log}\,x-2^{-1}\mathrm{log}\,\pi
and
G(x)=\exp\{-\exp(-x)\}
.

In practice, the sparsity level of the potential signal is always unknown. To accommodate both sparse and dense alternatives, we adopt the
\max\text{-}L_{\infty}
-type statistic proposed by
Wang and Feng 2023
for detecting sparse signals, and the
\max\text{-}L_{2}
-type statistic for identifying dense signals. We then integrate the
\max\text{-}L_{\infty}
-type and
\max\text{-}L_{2}
-type methods to construct a more powerful testing procedure. Following
Wang and Feng 2023
, we introduce the following additional assumptions to highlight the key steps and streamline the proof.

A
SSUMPTION
3

The components of
\boldsymbol{Z}_{i}
are independent sub-Gaussian variables, that is, there exists a constant
\kappa_{0}
, such that
\mathrm{sup}_{\ell\geq 1}\ell^{-1/2}\{\mathbb{E}(Z_{ij}^{\ell})\}^{1/\ell}\leq\kappa_{0}
,
i=1,\ldots,n,j=1,\ldots,p
.

The following theorem establishes the asymptotic independence between the
\max\text{-}L_{\infty}
-type and
\max\text{-}L_{2}
-type statistics under the null hypothesis
H_{0}
.

T
HEOREM
5

Suppose
H_{0}
and Assumptions
1
–
3
and Assumptions 1–2 in
Wang and Feng 2023
hold. If
\mathrm{log}n=o(p^{1/4})
,
p=o(n^{7/4})
, we have

(i)

As
(n,p)\rightarrow\infty
,
S_{n,p}
is asymptotically independent of
M_{n,p}
, that is

\displaystyle\mathrm{P}\left(S_{n,p}\leq\hat{\omega}x,~M_{n,p}\leq u_{p}\{\exp(-y)\}\right)\rightarrow F_{V}(x)\cdot\exp{\{-\exp(-x)\}};

(ii)

If
\lambda_{n}\sim n^{\lambda}
for some
\lambda\in(0,1)
, then, as
(n,p)\to\infty
,
S_{n,p}
is asymptotically independent of
M^{\dagger}_{n,p}
, that is

\displaystyle\mathrm{P}\left(S_{n,p}\leq\hat{\omega}x,~M^{\dagger}_{n,p}\leq\nu_{p,n}(y)\right)\rightarrow F_{V}(x)\cdot\exp{\{-\exp(-x)\}},

where
\nu_{p,n}(y)=\{y+D(p\mathrm{log}h_{n})\}/A(p\mathrm{log}h_{n})
.

Following the independence between
S_{n,p}
and
M_{n,p}
or
M^{\dagger}_{n,p}
induced by Theorem
5
, the Minimum combination, Fisher combination, and Cauchy combination can be used to combine the max-
L_{2}
-type test and max-
L_{\infty}
-type test, see
Wishart 1932
;
Littell and Folks 1971
;
Liu and Xie 2020
.
Long et al. 2023
have showed that the power of the test based on Cauchy combination would be more powerful than the Minimum combination test. Attracted by the stability of the Cauchy combination method, in this paper, we combine
S_{n,p}
with
M_{n,p}
and
M^{\dagger}_{n,p}
using the Cauchy combination test procedure in
Liu and Xie 2020
, that is,

\displaystyle T_{CC}=0.5\tan\{(0.5-p_{S_{n,p}})\pi\}+0.5\tan\{(0.5-p_{M_{n,p}})\pi\},

(5)

\displaystyle T_{CC}^{\dagger}=0.5\tan\{(0.5-p_{S_{n,p}})\pi\}+0.5\tan\{(0.5-p_{M^{\dagger}_{n,p}})\pi\}.

(6)

Since
T_{CC}
and
T_{CC}^{\dagger}
converge to a standard Cauchy distribution under
H_{0}
,

\displaystyle p_{CC}=1-F(T_{CC}),\quad p_{CC}^{\dagger}=1-F(T_{CC}^{\dagger}),

(7)

where
F(x)
is the cumulative distribution function of standard Cauchy distribution
C(0,1)
. Both
p_{CC}
and
p_{CC}^{\dagger}
can be used as the final
p
-values for testing the null hypothesis
H_{0}
. If the combined
p
-value is smaller than the pre-specified significance level
\alpha\in(0,1)
, we reject the null hypothesis.

We also give the asymptotic independence under the local alternative hypothesis, to wit,

\displaystyle\begin{gathered}H_{1;n,p}:\ \ |\mathcal{A}|=o\{p/(\mathrm{log}n)^{2}\}\ \text{and}\ \|{\boldsymbol{\delta}}\|^{2}=o\{n^{-1}\mathrm{tr}({\bf\Omega}_{n}^{2})\},\end{gathered}

where
\mathcal{A}=\{1\leq j\leq p:\delta_{j}\neq 0\}
is the support of
{\boldsymbol{\delta}}
.

T
HEOREM
6

Suppose Assumptions
1
–
3
, Assumptions 1–2 in
Wang and Feng 2023
and
\max_{1\leq j\leq p}|\widehat{\sigma}_{j}-\sigma_{j}|=o(1)
hold. Under
H_{1;n,p}
, if
p=o(n^{7/4})
, we have

(i)

As
(n,p)\rightarrow\infty
,
S_{n,p}
is asymptotically independent of
M_{n,p}
, that is

\displaystyle\mathrm{P}\left(S_{n,p}\leq\hat{\omega}x,~M_{n,p}\leq u_{p}\{\exp(-y)\}\right)\rightarrow\mathrm{P}\left(S_{n,p}\leq\hat{\omega}x\right)\mathrm{P}\left(M_{n,p}\leq u_{p}\{\exp(-y)\}\right);

(ii)

If
\lambda_{n}\sim n^{\lambda}
for some
\lambda\in(0,1)
, then, as
(n,p)\to\infty
,
S_{n,p}
is asymptotically independent of
M^{\dagger}_{n,p}
, that is

\displaystyle\mathrm{P}\left(S_{n,p}\leq\hat{\omega}x,~M^{\dagger}_{n,p}\leq\nu_{p,n}(y)\right)\rightarrow\mathrm{P}\left(S_{n,p}\leq\hat{\omega}x\right)\mathrm{P}\left(M^{\dagger}_{n,p}\leq\nu_{p,n}(y)\right),

where
\nu_{p,n}(y)=\{y+D(p\mathrm{log}h_{n})\}/A(p\mathrm{log}h_{n})
.

Theorem
6
enables a finer analysis of the power profiles of the proposed adaptive tests, moving beyond the guarantee of consistency. A key question that arises is the power performance of our adaptive procedure relative to non-adaptive approaches that rely on a single test statistic (either the max-
L_{2}
-type test or max-
L_{\infty}
-type test).
We compare the power of these two adaptive tests to their non-adaptive counterparts.
For a given significant level
\alpha\in(0,1)
, let
\beta_{S,\alpha}
,
\beta_{M,\alpha}
and
\beta_{CC,\alpha}
denote the power functions of the test
S_{n,p}
,
M_{n,p}
and the corresponding adaptive test. That is

\displaystyle\beta_{CC,\alpha}\geq\beta_{M\wedge S,\alpha}=

\displaystyle\mathrm{P}(\mathrm{min}\{p_{S_{n,p}},p_{M_{n,p}}\}\leq 1-\sqrt{1-\alpha})

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\mathrm{P}(\mathrm{min}\{p_{S_{n,p}},p_{M_{n,p}}\}\leq\alpha/2)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\beta_{M,\alpha/2}+\beta_{S,\alpha/2}-\mathrm{P}(p_{S_{n,p}}\leq\alpha/2,p_{M_{n,p}}\leq\alpha/2)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\max\{\beta_{M,\alpha/2},\beta_{S,\alpha/2}\}.

On the other hand, under
H_{1;n,p}
in (
3
), we have

\displaystyle\beta_{CC,\alpha}\geq\beta_{M,\alpha/2}+\beta_{S,\alpha/2}-\beta_{M,\alpha/2}\beta_{S,\alpha/2}+o_{p}(1).

For a small
\alpha
, the difference between
\beta_{S,\alpha/2}
and
\beta_{S,\alpha}
should be small. We conclude that the power of the adaptive test would be no smaller than that of either the max-
L_{2}
-type test or max-
L_{\infty}
-type test.

4
Location estimation

If the null hypothesis is rejected, it is of interest to detect the locations of the changepoints. We propose the following adaptive estimation procedure:

\hat{\tau}:=\begin{cases}\hat{\tau}_{S}:=\underset{1\leq k\leq n-1}{\arg\max}\{W(k)-\hat{\mu}_{M,k}\},&\text{ if }p_{S_{n,p}}<p_{M_{n,p}},\\
\hat{\tau}_{M}:=\underset{1\leq k\leq n-1}{\arg\max}\max_{j=1,\ldots,p}|C_{0,j}(k)|^{2},&\text{ otherwise, }\end{cases}

or

\hat{\tau}^{\dagger}:=\begin{cases}\hat{\tau}_{S}:=\underset{1\leq k\leq n-1}{\arg\max}\{W(k)-\hat{\mu}_{M,k}\},&\text{ if }p_{S_{n,p}}<p_{M_{n,p}^{\dagger}},\\
\hat{\tau}_{M^{\dagger}}:=\underset{\lambda_{n}\leq k\leq n-\lambda_{n}}{\arg\max}\max_{j=1,\ldots,p}|C_{0.5,j}(k)|^{2},&\text{ otherwise.}\end{cases}

Similar to the proof of Theorem 2.1 in
Jin et al. 2016
and Theorem 6 in
Wang and Feng 2023
, we establish the following theorem on the consistency of changepoint estimation under dependence.

T
HEOREM
7

Under the Assumptions
1
–
3
, assumptions in Theorem
2
, Assumptions 1–2 in
Wang and Feng 2023
and
\max_{1\leq j\leq p}|\widehat{\sigma}_{j}-\sigma_{j}|=o(1)
, if
M=\lceil(n\wedge p)^{1/8}\rceil
, we have
(\hat{\tau}-\tau)/n=o_{p}(1)
or
(\hat{\tau}^{\dagger}-\tau)/n=o_{p}(1)
, provided that either
\|\boldsymbol{\delta}\|_{\infty}/\sqrt{\mathrm{log}(np)/n}\rightarrow\infty
or
n\|{\boldsymbol{\delta}}\|^{2}\rightarrow\infty
, as
(n,p)\rightarrow\infty
.

5
Simulation Results

In this section, we conduct a comparative analysis of our proposed methods against the test procedures presented by
Li et al. 2019
(hereinafter referred to as LXZL) and
Wang et al. 2022
(hereinafter referred to as WZVS). Specifically, for the WZVS test, we set the parameter
\eta
to 0.02 as per their method. In addition to the aforementioned comparisons, we further evaluated our methods against two other approaches that rely on the assumption of independence and identical distribution—specifically, the methods proposed by
Jin et al. 2016
(hereinafter referred to as JPYZ) and
Wang et al. 2019
(hereinafter referred to as WZWY).

Additionally, to ensure consistency and comparability, we set the parameter
M=\lceil n^{1/8}\wedge p^{1/8}\rceil
in our method
S_{n,p}
. Given the convergence rate characteristics of the max-
L_{2}
-type and max-
L_{\infty}
-type test procedures, we normalize our test statistics
S_{n,p}
,
M_{n,p}
, and
M_{n,p}^{\dagger}
by dividing them by
1+n^{-2/3}\mathrm{log}p
in practical applications.

We generate samples from the model with

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{X}_{i}={\boldsymbol{\delta}}\mathbb{I}(i>\tau)+\sum\limits_{h=0}^{M_{0}}\mathbf{A}_{h}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{i-h},\ i=1,\ldots,n,

where
\mathbf{A}_{0},\dots,\mathbf{A}_{M_{0}}
are
p\times p
matrices which determine the autocovariance structure,
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{i}={\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}^{1/2}\boldsymbol{u}_{i}
,
\boldsymbol{u}_{i}=(u_{i1},\cdots,u_{ip})^{\top}
and
u_{ij}
are assumed to be generated independently and identically from
N(0,1)
and
t(4)
. This generating model implied
\mathrm{cov}(\boldsymbol{X}_{i},\boldsymbol{X}_{i+h})=\boldsymbol{\Gamma}(h)
for all
h\in\{-M_{0},\dots,0,\dots,M_{0}\}
, where
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}(h)=\sum\nolimits_{k=0}^{M_{0}-h}\mathbf{A}_{k}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}\mathbf{A}_{k+h}^{{\!\top\!}}
. The matrices
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}=\mathbf{I}_{p}
or
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}=(\rho^{i-j})_{p\times p}
, and
\mathbf{A}_{h}=(a_{h,ij})_{p\times p}
is given by

\mathbf{A}_{h}(i,j)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\dfrac{\phi}{h},&\mbox{if}~|i-j|=0,\\
\dfrac{\phi}{h|i-j|^{2}},&\mbox{if}~1\leq|i-j|\leq\lfloor pv\rfloor,\\
0,&\mbox{if}~|i-j|>\lfloor pv\rfloor,\end{array}\right.

for
h=1,\dots,M_{0}
. Here,
\phi
control the dependence among variables, and
v
stands for the sparsity level of matrices
\mathbf{A}_{h}
. Specially, we set
\mathbf{A}_{0}=\mathbf{I}_{p}
. The dependency level
M_{0}=0,2
.
We consider the following two scenarios for the covariance matrix:

(S1)

{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}=\mathbf{I}_{p}
,
\phi=0.5,v=0.5
;

(S2)

{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}=(0.5^{|i-j|})_{1\leq i,j\leq p}
,
\phi=0.2,v=0.2
.

Each scenario’s empirical size and power are evaluated over 500 Monte Carlo replications, with a nominal significance level of
\alpha=5\%
. For evaluating the performance of
S_{n,p}
test, we taking
T_{d}=B=10000
in Remark 2.3 and get
\hat{c}=0.9345
.

Tables
1
–
2
present the empirical sizes of each test under normal errors and
t(4)
errors, respectively. The results indicate that all the tests based on temporal dependence–
S_{n,p},M_{n,p},M_{n,p}^{\dagger}
, LXZL and WZVS generally exhibit excellent control over the empirical sizes across most scenarios. This finding underscores the robustness of our proposed methods, which are capable of maintaining accurate size control even in the presence of high-dimensional time series data with temporal dependence. This is particularly noteworthy given the complexities associated with high-dimensional settings and the potential challenges posed by temporal dependence, which can often lead to inflated Type I error rates in those testing procedures based on the independence assumption—JPYZ and WZWY. Our methods, therefore, demonstrate a high degree of reliability and applicability in such intricate and realistic data contexts.

For the power comparison, we specifically set the alternative parameters as follows:

\delta_{i}=c_{\tau}\sqrt{\frac{\mathrm{log}p}{ns}},\quad\text{for }i=1,\cdots,s,\quad\text{and}\quad\delta_{i}=0\quad\text{for }i>s.

Here, the parameter
s
denotes the sparsity level of the alternative hypotheses. We set
s=1,2,3,5,7,9,20,30,40,50
. For
\tau=0.5n
, we set
c_{\tau}=15
. While for
\tau=0.3n
, we set
c_{\tau}=20
. In this analysis, we focus on the scenario with a sample size
n=400
and dimension
p=500
. We note that the results for other combinations of sample sizes and dimensions exhibit similar trends.
Furthermore, we restrict our analysis to time series data with temporal dependence, specifically the case where
M_{0}=2
. The methods JYPZ and WZWY are excluded from the power comparison due to their significant size distortions, which compromise the validity of their power assessments.

Figure 1
:
Power curves of each test under Scenario (S2) and
\tau=0.3n
.

Figure 2
:
Power curves of each test under Scenario (S2) and
\tau=0.5n
.

Figures
1
–
2
present the empirical power curves of each test under two scenarios for the change-point location:
\tau=0.3n
and
\tau=0.5n
, respectively. Several important findings can be drawn from these results.

First, among the sum-type test procedures, our proposed test statistic
S_{n,p}
consistently outperforms the WZVS and LXZL tests across all settings, aligning with prior results established under independent observations, see
Wang and Feng 2023
. This confirms the robustness and efficiency of
S_{n,p}
, even in the presence of temporal dependence. Moreover, we observe that the max-
L_{2}
strategy underlying
S_{n,p}
yields better power than the conventional sum-
L_{2}
-based strategies, demonstrating its advantage in aggregating signals across dimensions.

Second, in scenarios characterized by sparse alternatives—where only a small subset of components exhibits structural change—the max-type test statistics
M_{n,p}
and
M_{n,p}^{\dagger}
demonstrate significantly higher power compared to sum-type procedures. This is expected, as max-type tests are specifically tailored to detect large deviations in a few components. In contrast, under dense alternatives—where many components change simultaneously with relatively small magnitudes—the sum-type tests, particularly
S_{n,p}
, show superior power performance.

Third, the proposed Cauchy combination test procedures, which integrate both max- and sum-type information, achieve strong adaptivity across varying levels of sparsity. These combination tests consistently deliver near-optimal or optimal power in all settings, with particularly strong performance in intermediate sparsity regimes where neither extreme approach is optimal on its own. This highlights the practical advantage of the adaptive strategy.

Lastly, it is worth noting a subtle but important distinction between the two max-type statistics:
M_{n,p}
tends to outperform
M_{n,p}^{\dagger}
when the change-point is located near the center of the time series (e.g.,
\tau=0.5n
), while the reverse is true when the change-point is closer to the boundary (e.g.,
\tau=0.3n
). This behavior is consistent with findings in the change-point literature, reflecting the sensitivity of max-type statistics to boundary effects and their dependency on variance estimation near the endpoints.

Figure 3
:
Estimation accuracy curves of each test under Scenario (S2) and
\tau=0.3n
.

Figure 4
:
Estimation accuracy curves of each test under Scenario (S2) and
\tau=0.5n
.

We next turn to the single change point estimation problem. Figures
3
–
4
present the estimation accuracy, measured by the absolute distance between the estimated and true changepoints, scaled by the sample size
n
. Each scenario’s accuracy are evaluated over 500 Monte Carlo replications.

The results show that our proposed estimator outperforms the LXZL method across all settings. The WZVS method exhibits a slight advantage, if the change-point is located near the center of the time series. However, when the change-point is near the boundaries, its estimation error increases significantly and exceeds that of our method. In contrast, our estimator demonstrates robust performance with respect to both the tail heaviness of the data distribution and the location of the change-point. Notably, the max-
L_{2}
aggregation strategy adopted in the
S_{n,p}
test statistic consistently achieves higher estimation accuracy than the traditional sum-
L_{2}
strategy, highlighting its advantage in aggregating signals under high dimensionality.

Under sparse alternatives, the max-type statistics
M_{n,p}
and
M_{n,p}^{\dagger}
yield significantly better estimation accuracy than sum-based methods. This finding aligns with the testing results, as max-type procedures are designed to detect large deviations in a few components. In contrast, under dense alternatives, sum-based estimators, particularly
S_{n,p}
, demonstrate superior accuracy. Similarly, our proposed Cauchy combination estimators exhibit strong adaptability across varying levels of sparsity. This highlights the practical importance of adaptive strategies in change-point estimation.

Overall, all the results confirm that our proposed methods—especially the Cauchy-type combination tests—offer a powerful and flexible framework for high-dimensional change-point inference under temporal dependence.

6
Real Data Application

In this section, we analyze financial data from the NASDAQ stock market, one of the most active equity markets globally. The NASDAQ, with its high concentration of technology and growth-oriented companies, provides a valuable platform for studying market dynamics. Its electronic trading structure and diverse listings offer a rich empirical basis for examining volatility, structural changes, and cross-sectional dependencies in modern financial markets.

In this paper, we consider the weekly closing prices of NASDAQ-listed constituent stocks over the period from January 2016 to January 2025. For each stock, we compute the corresponding weekly logarithmic return, defined as the difference between the natural logarithms of consecutive weekly closing prices, yielding 470 observations per stock. To ensure the comparability and completeness of the dataset, we retain only those firms that were continuously traded throughout the entire sample period, resulting in 1555 stocks. The log-return transformation is employed to stabilize the variance and capture proportional changes in prices, which are commonly used in empirical studies of financial returns.

We begin by examining whether serial dependence exists in the log-return series. Specifically, the Ljung–Box test
(
Ljung and Box 1978
)
for zero autocorrelation is applied to each stock series. At the
\alpha=5\%
significance level, a subset of the stocks exhibits statistically significant temporal dependence, while more rejections are observed when the level is relaxed to
\alpha=5\%
. The histogram of the resulting p-values is displayed in Figure
5
, suggesting that temporal dependence cannot be ignored. Furthermore, we employ the adaptive high-dimensional white noise test, as described in
Feng et al. 2022
, to verify if the residuals are white noise. The
p
-value of this test is 2e-26. This motivates us to employ subsequent inference procedures that explicitly accommodate such dependence structures.

Figure 5
:
Histogram of
p
-value of NASDAQ constituent stocks.

Table 3
:
The
p
-values for testing changepoints in weekly log-return rates.

Table
3
summarizes the
p
-values for testing changepoints in the weekly return rates. At the 5% significance level, the WZVS and LXZL tests fail to reject the null hypothesis. In contrast, the sum-type
S_{n,p}
, the max-type
M_{n,p}
,
M_{n,p}^{\dagger}
, and their Cauchy-combination type tests yield substantially smaller
p
-values, leading to a rejection of the null and indicating the presence of at least one changepoint in the log-return rates. Notably, all the proposed test statistics reject the null hypothesis at the 5% significance level, consistently indicating the presence of at least one changepoint in the NASDAQ log-return data. This collective evidence suggests that the market experienced structural shifts over the examined period. These results suggest that significant transitions occurred in market volatility or dependence structure, motivating further investigation into their timing and underlying economic drivers.

Building on this overall rejection, we proceed to single change-point detection. All methods identified change point, occurring around June 2022, March 2023, February 2021, June 2022 and March 2023 for
M_{n,p},M_{n,p}^{\dagger},S_{n,p},T_{CC}
and
T_{CC}^{\dagger}
. The change-point in February 2021 corresponds to a dense structural transition, reflecting the post-pandemic period of abundant liquidity and the subsequent correction triggered by the sharp rise in U.S. Treasury yields, broadly affecting technology and growth stocks. In contrast, the change-points in June 2022 and March 2023 represent sparse shifts, with the former linked to the Federal Reserve’s accelerated rate hikes and quantitative tightening, and the latter associated with the collapse of Silicon Valley Bank, primarily impacting banking and technology-related sectors. These results align with economic events and demonstrate that our method can effectively detect both global and local structural changes in high-dimensional financial data.

7
Conclusion

In this paper, we first propose a max-
L_{2}
-type test designed for detecting dense alternatives in high-dimensional change-point inference under temporal dependence. Building on this, we construct two Cauchy combination tests based on the established asymptotic independence between the proposed max-
L_{2}
-type test and existing max-
L_{\infty}
-type tests. Simulation studies and a real data application demonstrate the advantages of our adaptive procedures across a wide range of sparsity levels in the alternatives.

There remain several important directions for future research. First, our current framework assumes the observed time series follows a linear process. It would be valuable to extend the methodology to more general dependence structures, such as those satisfying
\alpha
-mixing or
\beta
-mixing conditions. Second, accurate estimation of the temporal dependence parameters—specifically the bandwidth
M
and the long-run variance—remains a challenging task in change-point detection and warrants further investigation.

8
Appendix

8.1
Notation

For the convenience of proof, we reformulate

\displaystyle W(k)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{k^{2}(n-k)^{2}}{n^{3}\sqrt{p}}\left(\frac{1}{k}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\boldsymbol{X}_{i}-\frac{1}{n-k}\sum_{i=k+1}^{n}\boldsymbol{X}_{i}\right)^{\top}\left(\frac{1}{k}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\boldsymbol{X}_{i}-\frac{1}{n-k}\sum_{i=k+1}^{n}\boldsymbol{X}_{i}\right).

Let
M=\lceil(n\wedge p)^{1/8}\rceil
, we define a
M
-dependent approximation sequence for
\{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
as

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{i}^{(M)}:=\mathbb{E}(\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{i}\mid\boldsymbol{Z}_{i-M},\dots,\boldsymbol{Z}_{i})={\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{1/2}\sum\limits_{\ell=0}^{M}b_{\ell}\boldsymbol{Z}_{i-\ell}.

In order to simplify notation, we omit the superscript in the absence of ambiguity. Furthermore, we denote the auto-covariance matrix of
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{i}
at lag
h
,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{M}(h):=\mathbb{E}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{i}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{i+h}^{\top})=c_{h,M}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}},

where
c_{h,M}=\sum\nolimits_{\ell=0}^{M-h}b_{\ell}b_{\ell+h}
. Let
\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{n,M}:=\sum_{h\in\mathcal{M}}(1-\frac{|h|}{n})\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{M}(h)
, where
\mathcal{M}=\{0,\pm 1,\dots,\pm M\}
.
Let
\{{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
be a Gaussian sequence which is independent of
\{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
and preserves the auto-covariance structure, that is,
\mathbb{E}({\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{i}{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{i+h}^{\top})=\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{M}(h)
. By replacing
\boldsymbol{X}_{i}
in
W(k)
with
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{i}
, we define

\displaystyle W(k)^{(NG)}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\frac{k^{2}(n-k)^{2}}{n^{3}\sqrt{p}}\left(\frac{1}{k}\sum_{i=1}^{k}{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}_{i}-\frac{1}{n-k}\sum_{i=k+1}^{n}{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}_{i}\right)^{\top}\left(\frac{1}{k}\sum_{i=1}^{k}{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}_{i}-\frac{1}{n-k}\sum_{i=k+1}^{n}{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}_{i}\right).

Similarly, we define

\displaystyle W(k)^{(G)}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\frac{k^{2}(n-k)^{2}}{n^{3}\sqrt{p}}\left(\frac{1}{k}\sum_{i=1}^{k}{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{i}-\frac{1}{n-k}\sum_{i=k+1}^{n}{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{i}\right)^{\top}\left(\frac{1}{k}\sum_{i=1}^{k}{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{i}-\frac{1}{n-k}\sum_{i=k+1}^{n}{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{i}\right).

We restate some of the notations and assumptions from
Wang and Feng 2023
that are referred to in this paper, which primarily concern the temporal and cross-sectional dependence structures of the noise terms
\epsilon_{ij}
. Suppose there exist measurable functions
g_{j}
’s such that
\epsilon_{ij}=g_{j}(e_{i},e_{i-1},\ldots)
, where
\{e_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}
is a sequence of independent and identically distributed (i.i.d.) random variables. To measure temporal dependence, define for
q\geq 1
,

\displaystyle a_{i}(q)=\limsup_{p\to\infty}\max_{j=1,\ldots,p}\|g_{j}(e_{i},e_{i-1},\ldots,e_{0},e_{-1},\ldots)-g_{j}(e_{i},e_{i-1},\ldots,e^{\prime}_{0},e_{-1},\ldots)\|_{q},

where
\{e^{\prime}_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}
is an independent copy of
\{e_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}
.
Let
\sigma_{jj^{\prime}}=\lim_{n\to\infty}n^{-1}\{\mathbb{E}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{i^{\prime}=1}^{n}\epsilon_{ij}\epsilon_{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}}\right)\}
be the long-run covariances, and denote
\sigma_{j}=\sigma_{jj}^{1/2}
. The componentwise correlations among
p
variables can thus be defined as
\rho_{jj^{\prime}}=\sigma_{jj^{\prime}}/(\sigma_{j}\sigma_{j^{\prime}})
. Denote
{\bf R}=(\rho_{jj^{\prime}})_{p\times p}
.
Let for some sequences
\delta_{p}>0
and
\kappa_{p}>0
,
B_{p,j}=\{1\leq j^{\prime}\leq p:|\rho_{jj^{\prime}}|\geq\delta_{p}\}
and
C_{p}=\{1\leq j\leq p:|B_{p,j}|\geq p^{\kappa_{p}}\}
.

A
SSUMPTION
4
(Assumption 1 in
Wang and Feng 2023
)

There exist some constants
q>4
and
\mathfrak{a}>5/2
such that
a_{i}(q)\lesssim i^{-\mathfrak{a}}
. In addition,
\liminf_{p\to\infty}\mathrm{min}_{j=1,\ldots,p}\sigma_{j}\geq\underline{\sigma}
for some constant
\underline{\sigma}>0
.

A
SSUMPTION
5
(Assumption 2 in
Wang and Feng 2023
)

(i)
|\rho_{jj^{\prime}}|\leq\varrho
for
1\leq j\neq j^{\prime}\leq p
and some constant
\varrho\in(0,1)
; (ii)
|C_{p}|/p\to 0
for some
\delta_{p}=o\{(\mathrm{log}p)^{-1}\}
and
\kappa_{p}\to 0
, as
p\to\infty
.

8.2
Proof of Theorem
1

Without loss of generality, we assume
{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{i}:={\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{0}+{\boldsymbol{\delta}}\mathbb{I}(i>\tau)=\mathbf{0}
under
H_{0}
for
i=1,\dots,n
. Then,
\{\boldsymbol{X}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
is a process with zero mean and auto-covariance structure given by
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}(h)
, that is,
\mathbb{E}(\boldsymbol{X}_{i}\boldsymbol{X}_{i+h})=\boldsymbol{\Gamma}(h)=c_{h}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}
for
h=0,1,2,\dots
and
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}(h)=\boldsymbol{\Gamma}(-h)^{\top}
with
c_{h}=\sum\nolimits_{\ell=0}^{\infty}b_{\ell}b_{\ell+h}
.

Proof.

The skeleton of the proof can be divided into three sub-steps. Firstly, we prove the asymptotic distribution of
W(\lfloor nt\rfloor)^{(G)}
; secondly, we establish the asymptotic distribution of
W(\lfloor nt\rfloor)^{(NG)}
with Gaussian approximation approach; finally, we use
W(\lfloor nt\rfloor)^{(NG)}
to approximate
W(\lfloor nt\rfloor)
. Note that, the term
W(\lfloor nt\rfloor)-\mu_{M,\lfloor nt\rfloor}
can be divided into the following four parts,

\displaystyle W(\lfloor nt\rfloor)-\mu_{M,\lfloor nt\rfloor}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{W(\lfloor nt\rfloor)-W(\lfloor nt\rfloor)^{(NG)}\right\}+\left\{W(\lfloor nt\rfloor)^{(NG)}-W(\lfloor nt\rfloor)^{(G)}\right\}

\displaystyle+\left\{W(\lfloor nt\rfloor)^{(G)}-\mu_{t}^{(G)}\right\}+\left\{\mu_{t}^{(G)}-\mu_{M,\lfloor nt\rfloor}\right\},

where
\mu_{\lfloor nt\rfloor}^{(G)}:=\mathbb{E}\{W(\lfloor nt\rfloor)^{(G)}\}
.
According to Lemmas
8.1
–
8.6
and Slutsky’s Theorem, we will complete the proof of Theorem 1.

L
EMMA
8.1

Under Assumptions
1
–
2
and
H_{0}
, we have

W(\lfloor nt\rfloor)^{(G)}-\mu_{\lfloor nt\rfloor}^{(G)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\rightarrow}}\omega V(t),\quad t\in[0,1],

where
V(t)
is a continuous Gaussian process with
\mathbb{E}\{V(t)\}=0
and

\mathbb{E}\{V(t)V(s)\}=(1-t)^{2}s^{2},\quad 0\leqslant s\leqslant t\leqslant 1.

Proof.

For
t\in[0,1]
and
i=1,\ldots,n
, we define

\displaystyle a_{i,t}:=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}\dfrac{1}{\lfloor nt\rfloor},&i\leq\lfloor nt\rfloor,\\
\dfrac{-1}{n-\lfloor nt\rfloor},&i>\lfloor nt\rfloor.\end{array}\right.

Define
g(k):=k^{2}(n-k)^{2}/(n^{3}\sqrt{p})
and let
k=\lfloor nt\rfloor
. We reformulate
W(\lfloor nt\rfloor)^{(G)}
as follows,

\displaystyle W(\lfloor nt\rfloor)^{(G)}

\displaystyle=g(\lfloor nt\rfloor)\sum\limits_{i,j=1}^{n}a_{i,t}a_{j,t}{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{i}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{j},

and

\displaystyle\mu_{\lfloor nt\rfloor}^{(G)}=g(\lfloor nt\rfloor)\sum\limits_{i,j=1}^{n}a_{i,t}a_{j,t}\mathrm{tr}\{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{M}(|j-i|)\}.

F_{t}^{(G)}
denotes an
n\times n
matrix whose
(i,j)
-th element is defined as

\displaystyle F_{ij,t}^{(G)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g(\lfloor nt\rfloor)a_{i,t}a_{j,t}\left[{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{i}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{j}-\mathrm{tr}\{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{M}(|j-i|)\}\right],

and hence

W(\lfloor nt\rfloor)^{(G)}-\mu_{\lfloor nt\rfloor}^{(G)}=\sum\limits_{i,j=1}^{n}F_{ij,t}^{(G)}.

Step 1.
We first consider a single time point
t
.

For establishing the asymptotic normality of
W(\lfloor nt\rfloor)^{(G)}-\mu_{t}^{(G)}
, we use the two-dimensional triangular arrays to divide it into three parts. For any
n
, choose
\alpha_{w}\in(0,1)
and
C>0
and
w_{n}=Cn^{\alpha_{w}}>M
, then
n=w_{n}k_{n}+r_{n}
. Here,
w_{n}
represents the size of each block,
k_{n}
represents the number of blocks constructed direction, and
r_{n}
stands for the points of the remainder terms which satisfy
0\leq r_{n}<w_{n}
. For any
i,j\in\{1,\dots,k_{n}\}
, define

\displaystyle B_{ij,t}^{(G)}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\ell_{1}=(i-1)w_{n}+1}^{iw_{n}-M}\sum\limits_{\ell_{2}=(j-1)w_{n}+1}^{jw_{n}-M}F^{(G)}_{\ell_{1}\ell_{2},t},

\displaystyle D_{ij,t}^{(G)}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\ell_{1}=(i-1)w_{n}+1}^{iw_{n}}\sum\limits_{\ell_{2}=(j-1)w_{n}+1}^{jw_{n}}F^{(G)}_{\ell_{1}\ell_{2},t}-B_{ij,t}^{G},

\displaystyle F_{t}^{(G)}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{(i,j)\in\{1,\dots,n\}^{2}/}\sum\limits_{\{1,\dots,w_{n}k_{n}\}^{2}}F^{(G)}_{ij,t}.

For non-Gaussian process
\{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
, we define
B_{ij,t}^{(NG)},D_{ij,t}^{(NG)},F_{t}^{(NG)},F^{(NG)}_{ij,t}
similarly. In this paper, we will use the unmarked symbol
F_{ij,t}
to represent
F_{ij,t}^{(G)}
or
F_{ij,t}^{(NG)}
when we do not emphasize the difference between Gaussian and non-Gaussian situation.
Here, we reformulate
W(\lfloor nt\rfloor)^{(G)}-\mu_{t}^{(G)}:=\mathcal{H}_{1,t}+\mathcal{H}_{2,t}+\mathcal{H}_{3,t}+\mathcal{H}_{4,t}
, where

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{1,t}

\displaystyle=2\sum\limits_{1\leq i<j\leq k_{n}}B_{ij,t},\quad\mathcal{H}_{2,t}=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{k_{n}}B_{ii,t},

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{3,t}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{1\leq i,j\leq k_{n}}D_{ij,t},\quad\mathcal{H}_{4,t}=F_{t}.

We will show

\displaystyle\omega^{-1}\mathcal{H}_{1,t}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\rightarrow}}V(t),\quad\quad\omega^{-1}(\mathcal{H}_{2,t}+\mathcal{H}_{3,t}+\mathcal{H}_{4,t})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\rightarrow}}0.

Step 1.1.
We now focus on
\mathcal{H}_{1,t}
. Before deriving the asymptotic distribution of
\mathcal{H}_{1,t}
, we define

{\boldsymbol{\eta}}_{i,t}:=a_{i,t}{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{i}
, and

\displaystyle\tilde{{\boldsymbol{\eta}}}_{i,t}=\frac{1}{w_{n}-M}\sum\limits_{\ell=(i-1)w_{n}+1}^{iw_{n}-M}{\boldsymbol{\eta}}_{\ell,t},

for
i=1,\dots,k_{n}
.
Then, for
i<j
,

\displaystyle B_{ij,t}=g(\lfloor nt\rfloor)(w_{n}-M)^{2}\tilde{{\boldsymbol{\eta}}}_{i,t}^{\top}\tilde{{\boldsymbol{\eta}}}_{j,t}.

Furthermore, we define

\displaystyle V_{nj,t}

\displaystyle:=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{j-1}B_{ij,t},~\mbox{for}~j=2,\dots,k_{n}

\displaystyle S_{nm,t}

\displaystyle:=\sum\limits_{j=2}^{m}V_{nj,t},~\mbox{for}~m=2,\dots,k_{n}.

For
m=2,\dots,k_{n}
,
\mathcal{F}_{nm}=\sigma(\tilde{{\boldsymbol{\eta}}}_{1,t},\dots,\tilde{{\boldsymbol{\eta}}}_{m,t})
denotes the
\sigma
-algebra generated by
\tilde{{\boldsymbol{\eta}}}_{1,t},\dots,\tilde{{\boldsymbol{\eta}}}_{m,t}
. Then, we have

\mathcal{H}_{1,t}=2S_{nk_{n},t}=2\sum\limits_{j=2}^{k_{n}}V_{nj,t}=2g(\lfloor nt\rfloor)(w_{n}-M)^{2}\sum\limits_{j=2}^{k_{n}}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{j-1}\tilde{{\boldsymbol{\eta}}}_{i,t}^{\top}\tilde{{\boldsymbol{\eta}}}_{j,t}.

Before deriving the asymptotic normality with Central limit theorems (CLT) of martingale differences, we need the following Propositions
8.1
–
8.3
.

P
ROPOSITION
8.1

For any
t\in[0,1]
,
\{V_{nm,t},m=2,\dots,k_{n}\}
is a martingale difference sequence with respect to the
\sigma
-algebra
\{\mathcal{F}_{nm},m=2,\dots,k_{n}\}
.

Proof.

We only need to prove
\{S_{nm,t},m=2,\dots,k_{n}\}
is a martingale sequence with respect to the
\sigma
-algebra
\{\mathcal{F}_{nm},m=2,\dots,k_{n}\}
for any
t\in[0,1]
.

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\{S_{n(m+1),t}|\mathcal{F}_{nm}\}

\displaystyle=g(\lfloor nt\rfloor)(w_{n}-M)^{2}\sum\limits_{j=2}^{m+1}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{j-1}\mathbb{E}\bigg(\tilde{{\boldsymbol{\eta}}}_{i,t}^{\top}\tilde{{\boldsymbol{\eta}}}_{j,t}|\mathcal{F}_{nm}\bigg)

\displaystyle=g(\lfloor nt\rfloor)(w_{n}-M)^{2}\bigg\{\sum\limits_{j=2}^{m}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{j-1}\tilde{{\boldsymbol{\eta}}}_{i,t}^{\top}\tilde{{\boldsymbol{\eta}}}_{j,t}+\sum\limits_{i=1}^{m}\tilde{{\boldsymbol{\eta}}}_{i,t}^{\top}\mathbb{E}(\tilde{{\boldsymbol{\eta}}}_{m+1,t})\bigg\}

\displaystyle=g(\lfloor nt\rfloor)(w_{n}-M)^{2}\bigg(\sum\limits_{j=2}^{m}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{j-1}\tilde{{\boldsymbol{\eta}}}_{i,t}^{\top}\tilde{{\boldsymbol{\eta}}}_{j,t}\bigg)=S_{nm,t}.

P
ROPOSITION
8.2

For any
t\in[0,1]
, we have

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{j=2}^{k_{n}}\mathbb{E}\bigg\{\frac{V_{nj,t}^{2}}{\omega^{2}}\bigg|\mathcal{F}_{n(j-1)}\bigg\}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\rightarrow}}\frac{t^{2}(1-t)^{2}}{4}.

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left\{V_{nj,t}^{2}|\mathcal{F}_{n(j-1)}\right\}

\displaystyle=\{g(\lfloor nt\rfloor)\}^{2}(w_{n}-M)^{4}\mathbb{E}\bigg\{\bigg(\sum\limits_{i=1}^{j-1}\tilde{{\boldsymbol{\eta}}}_{i,t}^{\top}\tilde{{\boldsymbol{\eta}}}_{j,t}\bigg)^{2}\bigg|\mathcal{F}_{n(j-1)}\bigg\}

\displaystyle=\{g(\lfloor nt\rfloor)\}^{2}(w_{n}-M)^{4}\sum\limits_{i_{1}=1}^{j-1}\sum\limits_{i_{2}=1}^{j-1}\tilde{{\boldsymbol{\eta}}}_{i_{1},t}^{\top}\mathcal{V}_{j,t}\tilde{{\boldsymbol{\eta}}}_{i_{2},t}.

Let
\varsigma_{n,t}:=\omega^{-2}\sum\nolimits_{j=2}^{k_{n}}\mathbb{E}\{V_{nj,t}^{2}|\mathcal{F}_{n(j-1),t}\}
, then

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}(\varsigma_{n,t})

\displaystyle=\{g(\lfloor nt\rfloor)\}^{2}(w_{n}-M)^{4}\omega^{-2}\sum\limits_{j=2}^{k_{n}}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{j-1}\mathrm{tr}(\mathcal{V}_{i,t}\mathcal{V}_{j,t}),

(10)

where
\mathcal{V}_{i,t}:=\mathrm{var}(\tilde{{\boldsymbol{\eta}}}_{i,t})
for
i=1,\dots,k_{n}
.
Let
b_{i}^{l}:=(i-1)w_{n}+1
and
b_{i}^{r}:=iw_{n}-M
be the left and right points of the
i
th large block
[b_{i}^{l},b_{i}^{r}]
, define

\displaystyle{\bf\Omega}_{w_{n}-M,M}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{h\in\mathcal{M}}\Big(1-\frac{|h|}{w_{n}-M}\Big)\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{M}(h).

When
\lfloor nt\rfloor<b_{i}^{l}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{i,t}=\frac{1}{w_{n}-M}\cdot\frac{1}{(n-\lfloor nt\rfloor)^{2}}{\bf\Omega}_{w_{n}-M,M}.

When
\lfloor nt\rfloor\geq b_{i}^{r}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{i,t}=\frac{1}{w_{n}-M}\cdot\frac{1}{\lfloor nt\rfloor^{2}}{\bf\Omega}_{w_{n}-M,M}.

When
b_{i}^{l}\leq\lfloor nt\rfloor<b_{i}^{r}
, we partition the
l
th large block
[b_{i}^{l},b_{i}^{r}]
into
[b_{i}^{l},\lfloor nt\rfloor]
and
[\lfloor nt\rfloor+1,b_{i}^{r}]
, and discuss
\mathcal{V}_{i,t}
with the following three sub-cases (i)–(iii).

(i)

When both
\lfloor nt\rfloor-b_{i}^{l}+1>M
and
b_{i}^{r}-\lfloor nt\rfloor>M
hold, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{i,t}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{w_{n}-M}\bigg\{\frac{1}{\lfloor nt\rfloor^{2}}\cdot\frac{\lfloor nt\rfloor-b_{i}^{l}+1}{w_{n}-M}\sum_{h\in\mathcal{M}}\bigg(1-\frac{|h|}{\lfloor nt\rfloor-b_{i}^{l}+1}\bigg)\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{M}(h)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{(n-\lfloor nt\rfloor)^{2}}\cdot\frac{b_{i}^{r}-\lfloor nt\rfloor}{w_{n}-M}\sum_{h\in\mathcal{M}}\bigg(1-\frac{|h|}{b_{i}^{r}-\lfloor nt\rfloor}\bigg)\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{M}(h)\bigg\}.

(11)

(ii)

When
\lfloor nt\rfloor-b_{i}^{l}+1\leq M
and
b_{i}^{r}-\lfloor nt\rfloor>M
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{i,t}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{w_{n}-M}\bigg\{\frac{1}{\lfloor nt\rfloor^{2}}\cdot\frac{\lfloor nt\rfloor-b_{i}^{l}+1}{w_{n}-M}\sum_{h=0,\pm 1,\cdots,\pm(\lfloor nt\rfloor-b_{i}^{l})}\bigg(1-\frac{|h|}{\lfloor nt\rfloor-b_{i}^{l}+1}\bigg)\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{M}(h)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{(n-\lfloor nt\rfloor)^{2}}\cdot\frac{b_{i}^{r}-\lfloor nt\rfloor}{w_{n}-M}\sum_{h\in\mathcal{M}}\bigg(1-\frac{|h|}{b_{i}^{r}-\lfloor nt\rfloor}\bigg)\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{M}(h)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{\lfloor nt\rfloor(n-\lfloor nt\rfloor)}\bigg(\sum_{h=\pm 1,\dots,\pm(\lfloor nt\rfloor-b_{i}^{l})}\frac{|h|}{w_{n}-M}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{M}(h)+\!\!\!\!\!\!\sum_{h=\pm(\lfloor nt\rfloor-b_{i}^{l}+1),\dots,\pm M}\!\!\!\!\!\!\frac{\lfloor nt\rfloor-b_{i}^{l}+1}{w_{n}-M}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{M}(h)\bigg)\bigg\}.

(12)

(iii)

When
\lfloor nt\rfloor-b_{i}^{l}+1>M
and
b_{i}^{r}-\lfloor nt\rfloor\leq M
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{i,t}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{w_{n}-M}\bigg\{\frac{1}{\lfloor nt\rfloor^{2}}\cdot\frac{\lfloor nt\rfloor-b_{i}^{l}+1}{w_{n}-M}\sum_{h\in\mathcal{M}}\bigg(1-\frac{|h|}{\lfloor nt\rfloor-b_{i}^{l}+1}\bigg)\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{M}(h)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{(n-\lfloor nt\rfloor)^{2}}\cdot\frac{b_{i}^{r}-\lfloor nt\rfloor}{w_{n}-M}\sum_{h=0,\pm 1,\cdots,\pm(b_{i}^{r}-\lfloor nt\rfloor-1)}\bigg(\frac{b_{i}^{r}-\lfloor nt\rfloor}{w_{n}-M}-\frac{|h|}{w_{n}-M}\bigg)\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{M}(h)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{\lfloor nt\rfloor(n-\lfloor nt\rfloor)}\bigg(\sum_{h=\pm 1,\cdots,\pm(b_{i}^{r}-\lfloor nt\rfloor)}\frac{|h|}{w_{n}-M}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{M}(h)+\!\!\!\!\!\!\sum_{h=\pm(b_{i}^{r}-\lfloor nt\rfloor+1),\cdots,\pm M}\!\!\frac{b_{i}^{r}-\lfloor nt\rfloor}{w_{n}-M}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{M}(h)\bigg)\bigg\}.

(13)

Now, we back to Equation (
10
) by considering two cases:

(I)

there exists
\ell_{n}
such that
\ell_{n}
th large block fall in the left side of the point
\lfloor nt\rfloor
and
(\ell_{n}+1)
th large block fall in the right side of the point
\lfloor nt\rfloor
, that is
b_{\ell_{n}}^{r}\leq\lfloor nt\rfloor<b_{\ell_{n}+1}^{l}
;

(II)

there exists
\ell_{n}
such that the point
\lfloor nt\rfloor
fall in the
\ell_{n}
th block, that is
b_{\ell_{n}}^{l}\leq\lfloor nt\rfloor<b_{\ell_{n}}^{r}
.

In case (I),
(\lfloor nt\rfloor-1-w_{n})/w_{n}<\ell_{n}<(\lfloor nt\rfloor+M)/w_{n}
, it means that
\lim_{n\to\infty}\ell_{n}w_{n}/n=t
and
\lim_{n\to\infty}(k_{n}-\ell_{n})w_{n}/n=1-t
. We have

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{j=2}^{k_{n}}\mathbb{E}(V_{nj,t}^{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{j=2}^{\ell_{n}}\mathbb{E}(V_{nj,t}^{2})+\sum\limits_{j=\ell_{n}+1}^{k_{n}}\mathbb{E}(V_{nj,t}^{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{g(\lfloor nt\rfloor)\}^{2}(w_{n}-M)^{4}\bigg(\sum\limits_{j=2}^{\ell_{n}}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{j-1}+\sum\limits_{j=\ell_{n}+1}^{k_{n}}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{\ell_{n}}+\sum\limits_{j=\ell_{n}+1}^{k_{n}}\sum\limits_{i=\ell_{n}+1}^{j-1}\bigg)\mathrm{tr}(\mathcal{V}_{i,t}\mathcal{V}_{j,t})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{g(\lfloor nt\rfloor)\}^{2}(w_{n}-M)^{2}\bigg\{\frac{1}{\lfloor nt\rfloor^{4}}\cdot\frac{\ell_{n}(\ell_{n}-1)}{2}+\frac{1}{\lfloor nt\rfloor^{2}(n-\lfloor nt\rfloor)^{2}}\cdot(k_{n}-\ell_{n})\ell_{n}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{(n-\lfloor nt\rfloor)^{4}}\cdot\frac{(k_{n}-\ell_{n})(k_{n}-\ell_{n}-1)}{2}\bigg\}\mathrm{tr}({\bf\Omega}_{w_{n}-M,M}^{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigg\{\frac{(n-\lfloor nt\rfloor)^{4}(w_{n}-M)^{2}}{n^{6}p}\cdot\frac{\ell_{n}(\ell_{n}-1)}{2}+\frac{\lfloor nt\rfloor^{2}(n-\lfloor nt\rfloor)^{2}(w_{n}-M)^{2}}{n^{6}p}\cdot(k_{n}-\ell_{n})\ell_{n}

\displaystyle+\frac{\lfloor nt\rfloor^{4}(w_{n}-M)^{2}}{n^{6}p}\cdot\frac{(k_{n}-\ell_{n})(k_{n}-\ell_{n}-1)}{2}\bigg\}\mathrm{tr}({\bf\Omega}_{w_{n}-M,M}^{2})

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle\bigg\{\frac{(1-t)^{4}t^{2}}{2}+\frac{2(1-t)^{3}t^{3}}{2}+\frac{(1-t)^{2}t^{4}}{2}\bigg\}\lim_{p\to\infty}\frac{\mathrm{tr}({\bf\Omega}_{w_{n}-M,M}^{2})}{p}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{t^{2}(1-t)^{2}}{2}\cdot\lim_{p\to\infty}\frac{\mathrm{tr}({\bf\Omega}_{w_{n}-M,M}^{2})}{p}.

Define
d_{n,M}:=\sum\nolimits_{h\in\mathcal{M}}(1-\frac{|h|}{n})c_{h}
and
d_{w_{n}-M,M}:=\sum\nolimits_{h\in\mathcal{M}}(1-\frac{|h|}{w_{n}-M})c_{h}
, by Assumption
1
-(ii) and
M=\lceil(n\wedge p)^{1/8}\rceil
, we have

\displaystyle d_{n,M}=\sum\limits_{h\in\mathcal{M}}\sum\limits_{\ell=0}^{\infty}b_{\ell}b_{\ell+h}-\frac{1}{n}\sum\limits_{h\in\mathcal{M}}|h|c_{h}\rightarrow s^{2},\quad\mbox{as}\quad n,p\rightarrow\infty

with the fact that
\sum\nolimits_{h=-\infty}^{\infty}|h||c_{h}|<\infty
. Similarly,
d_{w_{n}-M,M}\rightarrow s^{2}
as
n,p\rightarrow\infty
. Thus, as
n,p\rightarrow\infty
,

\displaystyle\frac{|\mathrm{tr}({\bf\Omega}_{w_{n}-M,M}^{2})-\mathrm{tr}({\bf\Omega}_{n,M}^{2})|}{\mathrm{tr}({\bf\Omega}_{n,M}^{2})}

\displaystyle=\bigg|\frac{d_{w_{n}-M,M}^{2}-d_{n,M}^{2}}{d_{n,M}^{2}}\bigg|=o(1).

Furthermore, by Lemma
8.5
–
8.6
, we have

\mathbb{E}(\varsigma_{n,t})=\omega^{-2}\sum\limits_{j=2}^{k_{n}}\mathbb{E}(V_{nj,t}^{2})\rightarrow\frac{t^{2}(1-t)^{2}}{4}.

Furthermore,

\displaystyle\mathrm{var}(\varsigma_{n,t})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}(\varsigma_{n,t}^{2})-\{\mathbb{E}(\varsigma_{n,t})\}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{g(\lfloor nt\rfloor)\}^{4}(w_{n}-M)^{8}\omega^{-4}\bigg\{\sum\limits_{j_{1},j_{2}=2}^{k_{n}}\sum\limits_{i_{1},i_{3}=1}^{j_{1}-1}\sum\limits_{i_{2},i_{4}=1}^{j_{2}-1}\mathbb{E}\bigg(\tilde{{\boldsymbol{\eta}}}_{i_{1},t}^{\top}\mathcal{V}_{j_{1},t}\tilde{{\boldsymbol{\eta}}}_{i_{3},t}\tilde{{\boldsymbol{\eta}}}_{i_{2},t}^{\top}\mathcal{V}_{j_{2},t}\tilde{{\boldsymbol{\eta}}}_{i_{4},t}\bigg)

\displaystyle-\sum\limits_{j_{1},j_{2}=2}^{k_{n}}\sum\limits_{i_{1}=1}^{j_{1}-1}\sum\limits_{i_{2}=1}^{j_{2}-1}\mathrm{tr}\bigg(\mathcal{V}_{i_{1},t}\mathcal{V}_{j_{1},t}\bigg)\mathrm{tr}\bigg(\mathcal{V}_{i_{2},t}\mathcal{V}_{j_{2},t}\bigg)\bigg\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{g(\lfloor nt\rfloor)\}^{4}(w_{n}-M)^{8}\omega^{-4}\bigg\{\sum\limits_{j_{1},j_{2}=2}^{k_{n}}\sum\limits_{i_{1}\neq i_{3}=1}^{j_{1}-1}\sum\limits_{i_{2}\neq i_{4}=1}^{j_{2}-1}\mathbb{E}\bigg(\tilde{{\boldsymbol{\eta}}}_{i_{1},t}^{\top}\mathcal{V}_{j_{1},t}\tilde{{\boldsymbol{\eta}}}_{i_{3},t}\tilde{{\boldsymbol{\eta}}}_{i_{2},t}^{\top}\mathcal{V}_{j_{2},t}\tilde{{\boldsymbol{\eta}}}_{i_{4},t}\bigg)\bigg\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{g(\lfloor nt\rfloor)\}^{4}(w_{n}-M)^{8}\omega^{-4}\bigg\{\sum\limits_{j_{1},j_{2}=2}^{k_{n}}\sum\limits_{i_{1}\neq i_{2}=1}^{j_{1}\wedge j_{2}-1}\mathrm{tr}(\mathcal{V}_{i_{1},t}\mathcal{V}_{j_{1},t}\mathcal{V}_{i_{2},t}\mathcal{V}_{j_{2},t})\bigg\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O\bigg\{\frac{n^{4}(w_{n}-M)^{8}}{p^{2}}\cdot\frac{p^{2}}{\mathrm{tr}^{2}({\bf\Omega}_{n,M}^{2})}\cdot\frac{k_{n}^{4}\mathrm{tr}({\bf\Omega}_{w_{n}-M,M}^{4})}{n^{8}(w_{n}-M)^{4}}\cdot\frac{\mathrm{tr}^{2}({\bf\Omega}_{n,M}^{2})}{\mathrm{tr}^{2}({\bf\Omega}_{n}^{2})}\bigg\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O\bigg\{\frac{\mathrm{tr}({\bf\Omega}_{w_{n}-M,M}^{4})}{\mathrm{tr}^{2}({\bf\Omega}_{n,M}^{2})}\bigg\}=O(p^{-1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle o(1),

the last equation holds because both
{\bf\Omega}_{w_{n}-M,M}
and
{\bf\Omega}_{n,M}
are bounded in spectral norm.
Above all,

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{j=2}^{k_{n}}\mathbb{E}\bigg\{\frac{V_{nj,t}^{2}}{\omega^{2}}\bigg|\mathcal{F}_{n(j-1),t}\bigg\}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\rightarrow}}\frac{t^{2}(1-t)^{2}}{4}.

In case (II), we follow the similar discussion of case (I). By Assumption
2
, considering Equations (
(i)
)–(
(iii)
), for
i=1,\dots,k_{n}
, we have
\lambda_{\max}(\mathcal{V}_{i,t})=O\{n^{-2}(w_{n}-M)^{-1}\}
, and
p^{-1}\mathrm{tr}({\bf\Omega}_{w_{n}-M,M}^{2})>d_{w_{n}-n,M}^{2}C_{0}^{2}
,
then

\displaystyle\bigg|\frac{\mathbb{E}(V_{n\ell_{n},t}^{2})}{p^{-1}\mathrm{tr}({\bf\Omega}_{w_{n}-M,M}^{2})}\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\{g(\lfloor nt\rfloor)\}^{2}(w_{n}-M)^{4}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{\ell_{n}-1}\bigg|\frac{\mathrm{tr}(\mathcal{V}_{i,t}\mathcal{V}_{\ell_{n},t})}{d_{w_{n}-n,M}^{2}C_{0}^{2}}\bigg|

\displaystyle=O\bigg\{\frac{n^{2}(w_{n}-M)^{4}(\ell_{n}-1)p}{pn^{4}(w_{n}-M)^{2}}\bigg\}=O\bigg(\frac{w_{n}}{n}\bigg)

\displaystyle=o(1).

Then

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}(V_{n\ell_{n},t}^{2})=o\bigg\{\frac{\mathrm{tr}({\bf\Omega}_{w_{n}-M,M}^{2})}{p}\bigg\}.

Thus,

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{j=2}^{k_{n}}\mathbb{E}(V_{nj,t}^{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{j=2}^{\ell_{n}-1}\mathbb{E}(V_{nj,t}^{2})+\mathbb{E}(V_{n\ell_{n},t}^{2})+\sum\limits_{j=\ell_{n}+1}^{k_{n}}\mathbb{E}(V_{nj,t}^{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\{g(\lfloor nt\rfloor)\}^{2}(w_{n}-M)^{2}}{\lfloor nt\rfloor^{4}}\sum\limits_{j=2}^{\ell_{n}-1}(j-1)\mathrm{tr}({\bf\Omega}_{w_{n}-M,M}^{2})

\displaystyle+\frac{\{g(\lfloor nt\rfloor)\}^{2}(w_{n}-M)^{2}}{\lfloor nt\rfloor^{2}(n-\lfloor nt\rfloor)^{2}}(k_{n}-\ell_{n})\ell_{n}\mathrm{tr}({\bf\Omega}_{w_{n}-M,M}^{2})

\displaystyle+\frac{\{g(\lfloor nt\rfloor)\}^{2}(w_{n}-M)^{2}}{(n-\lfloor nt\rfloor)^{4}}\sum\limits_{j=\ell_{n}+1}^{k_{n}}(j-\ell_{n}-1)\mathrm{tr}({\bf\Omega}_{w_{n}-M,M}^{2})+o\bigg\{\frac{\mathrm{tr}({\bf\Omega}_{w_{n}-M,M}^{2})}{p}\bigg\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(n-\lfloor nt\rfloor)^{4}(w_{n}-M)^{2}}{n^{6}p}\cdot\frac{(\ell_{n}-1)(\ell_{n}-2)}{2}\mathrm{tr}({\bf\Omega}_{w_{n}-M,M}^{2})

\displaystyle+\frac{\lfloor nt\rfloor^{2}(n-\lfloor nt\rfloor)^{2}(w_{n}-M)^{2}}{n^{6}p}(k_{n}-\ell_{n})\ell_{n}\mathrm{tr}({\bf\Omega}_{w_{n}-M,M}^{2})

\displaystyle+\frac{\lfloor nt\rfloor^{4}(w_{n}-M)^{2}}{n^{6}p}\cdot\frac{(k_{n}-\ell_{n})(k_{n}-\ell_{n}-1)}{2}\mathrm{tr}({\bf\Omega}_{w_{n}-M,M}^{2})+o\bigg\{\frac{\mathrm{tr}({\bf\Omega}_{w_{n}-M,M}^{2})}{p}\bigg\}

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle\bigg\{\frac{(1-t)^{4}t^{2}}{2}+\frac{2(1-t)^{3}t^{3}}{2}+\frac{(1-t)^{2}t^{4}}{2}+o(1)\bigg\}\lim_{p\to\infty}\frac{\mathrm{tr}({\bf\Omega}_{w_{n}-M,M}^{2})}{p}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{t^{2}(1-t)^{2}}{2}\cdot\lim_{p\to\infty}\frac{\mathrm{tr}({\bf\Omega}_{w_{n}-M,M}^{2})}{p}\{1+o(1)\}.

Then,

\mathbb{E}(\varsigma_{n,t})=\omega^{-2}\sum\limits_{j=2}^{k_{n}}\mathbb{E}(V_{nj,t}^{2})\rightarrow\frac{t^{2}(1-t)^{2}}{4}.

By taking similar arguments, we have
\mathrm{var}(\varsigma_{n,t})=o(1)
.
Now, Proposition
8.2
is finished.

P
ROPOSITION
8.3

For any
t\in[0,1]
and
\epsilon>0
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\omega^{2}}\sum\limits_{j=2}^{k_{n}}\mathbb{E}\bigg\{V_{nj,t}^{2}\mathbb{I}(|V_{nj,t}|>\omega\epsilon)\bigg|\mathcal{F}_{n(j-1)}\bigg\}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\rightarrow}}0.

Proof.

It suffices to show

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigg[\sum\limits_{j=2}^{k_{n}}\mathbb{E}\left\{V_{nj,t}^{4}|\mathcal{F}_{n(j-1)}\right\}\bigg]=o(\omega^{4}).

Due to the fact

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\omega^{2}}\sum\limits_{j=2}^{k_{n}}\mathbb{E}\{V_{nj,t}^{2}\mathbb{I}(|V_{nj,t}|>\omega\epsilon)|\mathcal{F}_{n(j-1)}\}\leq\frac{1}{\omega^{4}\epsilon^{2}}\sum\limits_{j=2}^{k_{n}}\mathbb{E}\{V_{nj,t}^{4}|\mathcal{F}_{n(j-1)}\},

we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigg[\sum\limits_{j=2}^{k_{n}}\mathbb{E}\{V_{nj,t}^{4}|\mathcal{F}_{n(j-1)}\}\bigg]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{g(\lfloor nt\rfloor)\}^{4}(w_{n}-M)^{8}\sum\limits_{j=2}^{k_{n}}\mathbb{E}\Big(\sum\limits_{i=1}^{j-1}\tilde{{\boldsymbol{\eta}}}_{i,t}^{\top}\tilde{{\boldsymbol{\eta}}}_{j,t}\Big)^{4}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{g(\lfloor nt\rfloor)\}^{4}(w_{n}-M)^{8}\sum\limits_{j=2}^{k_{n}}\mathbb{E}\Big(\sum\limits_{i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4}=1}^{j-1}\tilde{{\boldsymbol{\eta}}}_{i_{1},t}^{\top}\tilde{{\boldsymbol{\eta}}}_{j,t}\tilde{{\boldsymbol{\eta}}}_{i_{2},t}^{\top}\tilde{{\boldsymbol{\eta}}}_{j,t}\tilde{{\boldsymbol{\eta}}}_{i_{3},t}^{\top}\tilde{{\boldsymbol{\eta}}}_{j,t}\tilde{{\boldsymbol{\eta}}}_{i_{4},t}^{\top}\tilde{{\boldsymbol{\eta}}}_{j,t}\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{g(\lfloor nt\rfloor)\}^{4}(w_{n}-M)^{8}\sum\limits_{j=2}^{k_{n}}\mathbb{E}\Big\{3\sum\limits_{i_{1}\neq i_{2}}^{j-1}\mathrm{tr}(\mathcal{V}_{i_{1},t}\mathcal{V}_{j,t})\mathrm{tr}(\mathcal{V}_{i_{2},t}\mathcal{V}_{j,t})+\sum\limits_{i=1}^{j-1}\mathrm{tr}(\mathcal{V}_{i,t}\mathcal{V}_{j,t})^{2}\Big\}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{Cn^{4}(w_{n}-M)^{8}}{p^{2}}\cdot\frac{1}{n^{8}(w_{n}-M)^{4}}\bigg\{\frac{k_{n}(k_{n}+1)(2k_{n}+1)}{6}\mathrm{tr}^{2}({\bf\Omega}_{w_{n}-M,M}^{2})

\displaystyle+\frac{k_{n}(k_{n}-1)}{2}\mathrm{tr}({\bf\Omega}_{w_{n}-M,M}^{4})\bigg\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle o(\omega^{4}),

with
\mathrm{tr}({\bf\Omega}_{w_{n}-M,M}^{4})=o\{\mathrm{tr}^{2}({\bf\Omega}_{w_{n}-M,M}^{2})\}
.
Thus, the Lindberg condition of CLT is satisfied. Proposition
8.3
has been proved.

Step 1.2.

In this step, we will prove
\omega^{-1}(\mathcal{H}_{2,t}+\mathcal{H}_{3,t}+\mathcal{H}_{4,t})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\rightarrow}}0.

For the sake of proof, we reformulate

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{2,t}+\mathcal{H}_{3,t}+\mathcal{H}_{4,t}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{i=1}^{k_{n}}B_{ii,t}+\sum\limits_{i=1}^{k_{n}}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{k_{n}}D_{ij,t}+F_{t}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{i=1}^{k_{n}}\sum\limits_{\ell_{1}=(i-1)w_{n}+1}^{iw_{n}-M}\sum\limits_{\ell_{2}=(i-1)w_{n}+1}^{iw_{n}-M}F_{\ell_{1}\ell_{2},t}+2\sum\limits_{i=1}^{k_{n}}\Big(\sum\limits_{\ell_{1}=1}^{n}\sum\limits_{\ell_{2}=iw_{n}-M+1}^{iw_{n}}F_{\ell_{1}\ell_{2},t}\Big)

\displaystyle+2\sum\limits_{i=1}^{k_{n}}\Big(\sum\limits_{\ell_{1}=k_{n}w_{n}+1}^{n}\sum\limits_{\ell_{2}=(i-1)w_{n}+1}^{iw_{n}-M}F_{\ell_{1}\ell_{2},t}\Big)-\sum\limits_{i=1}^{k_{n}}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{k_{n}}\sum\limits_{\ell_{1}=iw_{n}-M+1}^{iw_{n}}\sum\limits_{\ell_{2}=jw_{n}-M+1}^{jw_{n}}F_{\ell_{1}\ell_{2},t}

\displaystyle+\sum\limits_{\ell_{1}=k_{n}w_{n}+1}^{n}\sum\limits_{\ell_{2}=k_{n}w_{n}+1}^{n}F_{\ell_{1}\ell_{2},t}.

Thus, we have
\mathbb{E}(F_{ij,t})=0
, and

\displaystyle\mathrm{cov}(F_{i_{1}j_{1},t},F_{i_{2}j_{2},t})

\displaystyle=\{g(\lfloor nt\rfloor)\}^{2}a_{i_{1},t}a_{j_{1},t}a_{i_{2},t}a_{j_{2},t}\big[\mathrm{tr}\{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{M}(|i_{2}-i_{1}|)\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{M}(|j_{2}-j_{1}|)\}

\displaystyle+\mathrm{tr}\{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{M}(|i_{1}-j_{2}|)\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{M}(|i_{2}-j_{1}|)\big].

Thus,
\mathbb{E}(B_{ii,t})=0
,
\mathrm{cov}(B_{ii,t},B_{jj,t})=0
for any
i\neq j
, and

\displaystyle\mathrm{var}\Big(\sum\limits_{i=1}^{k_{n}}B_{ii,t}\Big)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{k_{n}}\mathbb{E}\bigg(\sum\limits_{\ell_{1}=(i-1)w_{n}+1}^{iw_{n}-M}\sum\limits_{\ell_{2}=(i-1)w_{n}+1}^{iw_{n}-M}F_{\ell_{1}\ell_{2},t}\bigg)^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{k_{n}}\sum\limits_{\ell_{1},\ell_{1}^{{}^{\prime}},\ell_{2},\ell_{2}^{{}^{\prime}}=(i-1)w_{n}+1}^{iw_{n}-M}\mathbb{E}\bigg(F_{\ell_{1}\ell_{2},t}F_{\ell_{1}{{}^{\prime}}\ell_{2}{{}^{\prime}},t}\bigg)

\displaystyle=O\bigg\{\frac{k_{n}(w_{n}-M)^{2}\mathrm{tr}({\bf\Omega}_{w_{n}-M,M}^{2})}{n^{2}p}\bigg\}.

Recall
\omega=\lim_{p\to\infty}\sqrt{2\mathrm{tr}({\bf\Omega}^{2})/p}
and
M=\lceil(n\wedge p)^{1/8}\rceil
, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{var}\Big(\omega^{-1}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{k_{n}}B_{ii,t}\Big)

\displaystyle=O\bigg\{\frac{(w_{n}-M)\mathrm{tr}({\bf\Omega}_{w_{n}-M,M}^{2})}{n\mathrm{tr}({\bf\Omega}_{n}^{2})}\bigg\}=o(1).

Then, we have
\omega^{-1}\sum\nolimits_{i=1}^{k_{n}}B_{ii,t}=o_{p}(1)
.
Let
\mathcal{R}_{i,t}:=\sum\limits_{\ell_{1}=1}^{n}\sum\limits_{\ell_{2}=iw_{n}-M+1}^{iw_{n}}F_{\ell_{1}\ell_{2},t}
, for sufficiently large
n
, we have
\mathcal{R}_{i,t}
and
\mathcal{R}_{j,t}
are independent because
w_{n}
is greater than
M
.
Then,

\displaystyle\mathrm{var}\Big(\sum\limits_{i=1}^{k_{n}}\mathcal{R}_{i,t}\Big)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{k_{n}}\mathbb{E}\bigg(\sum\limits_{\ell_{1}=1}^{n}\sum\limits_{\ell_{2}=iw_{n}-M+1}^{iw_{n}}F_{\ell_{1}\ell_{2},t}\bigg)^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{k_{n}}\sum\limits_{\ell_{1}=1}^{n}\sum\limits_{\ell_{2}=iw_{n}-M+1}^{iw_{n}}\sum\limits_{\ell_{1}^{{}^{\prime}}=1}^{n}\sum\limits_{\ell_{2}^{{}^{\prime}}=iw_{n}-M+1}^{iw_{n}}\mathbb{E}\bigg(F_{\ell_{1}\ell_{2},t}F_{\ell_{1}{{}^{\prime}}\ell_{2}{{}^{\prime}},t}\bigg)

\displaystyle=O\bigg\{\frac{k_{n}nM\mathrm{tr}({\bf\Omega}_{n,M}{\bf\Omega}_{M,M})}{n^{2}p}\bigg\}.

Thus
\omega^{-1}\sum\nolimits_{i=1}^{k_{n}}\mathcal{R}_{i,t}=o_{p}(1)
.
Similarly, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{var}\bigg\{\omega^{-1}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{k_{n}}\Big(\sum\limits_{\ell_{1}=k_{n}w_{n}+1}^{n}\sum\limits_{\ell_{2}=(i-1)w_{n}+1}^{iw_{n}-M}F_{\ell_{1}\ell_{2},t}\Big)\bigg\}=o(1),

and

\displaystyle\mathrm{var}\bigg(\omega^{-1}\sum\limits_{\ell_{1}=k_{n}w_{n}+1}^{n}\sum\limits_{\ell_{2}=k_{n}w_{n}+1}^{n}F_{\ell_{1}\ell_{2},t}\bigg)=o(1).

Thus,
\mathcal{H}_{2,t}+\mathcal{H}_{3,t}+\mathcal{H}_{4,t}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\rightarrow}}0.
Following Propositions
8.1
–
8.3
, for fixed
t\in[0,1]
, we have

W(\lfloor nt\rfloor)^{(G)}-\mu_{\lfloor nt\rfloor}^{(G)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\rightarrow}}\omega N\left(0,t^{2}(1-t)^{2}\right).

Step 2.
We consider two time points
t
and
s
with
s<t
.
In this step, we consider the convergence in distribution of

\displaystyle\omega^{-1}\left[a\{W(\lfloor nt\rfloor)^{(G)}-\mu_{t}^{(G)}\}+b\{W(\lfloor ns\rfloor)^{(G)}-\mu_{s}^{(G)}\}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\omega^{-1}(a\mathcal{H}_{1,t}+b\mathcal{H}_{1,s})+o_{p}(1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\omega^{-1}\bigg(a\sum\limits_{j=2}^{k_{n}}V_{nj,t}+b\sum\limits_{j=2}^{k_{n}}V_{nj,s}\bigg)+o_{p}(1):=2\omega^{-1}\sum\limits_{j=2}^{k_{n}}\tilde{V}_{nj,ts}+o_{p}(1).

It is obvious that
\{\tilde{V}_{nm,ts},m=2,\dots,k_{n}\}
is a martingale difference sequence with respect to the
\sigma
-algebra
\{\mathcal{F}_{nm},m=2,\dots,k_{n}\}
. Now, we need to consider

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{j=2}^{k_{n}}\mathbb{E}(\tilde{V}_{nj,ts}^{2})

\displaystyle=a^{2}\sum\limits_{j=2}^{k_{n}}\mathbb{E}(V_{nj,t}^{2})+b^{2}\sum\limits_{j=2}^{k_{n}}\mathbb{E}(V_{nj,s}^{2})+2ab\sum\limits_{j=2}^{k_{n}}\mathbb{E}(V_{nj,t}V_{nj,s}),

and

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\{V_{nj,t}V_{nj,s}|\mathcal{F}_{n(j-1)}\}

\displaystyle=g(\lfloor nt\rfloor)g(\lfloor ns\rfloor)(w_{n}-M)^{4}\mathbb{E}\bigg\{\bigg(\sum\limits_{i=1}^{j-1}\tilde{{\boldsymbol{\eta}}}_{i,t}^{\top}\tilde{{\boldsymbol{\eta}}}_{j,t}\bigg)\bigg(\sum\limits_{i=1}^{j-1}\tilde{{\boldsymbol{\eta}}}_{i,s}^{\top}\tilde{{\boldsymbol{\eta}}}_{j,s}\bigg)\bigg|\mathcal{F}_{n(j-1)}\bigg\}

\displaystyle=g(\lfloor nt\rfloor)g(\lfloor ns\rfloor)(w_{n}-M)^{4}\sum\limits_{i_{1}=1}^{j-1}\sum\limits_{i_{2}=1}^{j-1}\tilde{{\boldsymbol{\eta}}}_{i_{1},t}^{\top}\mathcal{V}_{j,ts}\tilde{{\boldsymbol{\eta}}}_{i_{2},s},

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}(V_{nj,t}V_{nj,s})

\displaystyle=g(\lfloor nt\rfloor)g(\lfloor ns\rfloor)(w_{n}-M)^{4}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{j-1}\mathrm{tr}(\mathcal{V}_{i,ts}\mathcal{V}_{j,ts}),

where
\mathcal{V}_{i,ts}:=\mathbb{E}(\tilde{{\boldsymbol{\eta}}}_{i,t}\tilde{{\boldsymbol{\eta}}}_{i,s}^{\top})
, for
i=1,\dots,k_{n}
.
If
b_{i}^{r}\leq\lfloor ns\rfloor
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{i,ts}=\frac{1}{(w_{n}-M)}\cdot\frac{1}{\lfloor nt\rfloor\lfloor ns\rfloor}{\bf\Omega}_{w_{n}-M,M}.

If
b_{i}^{l}\leq\lfloor ns\rfloor<b_{i}^{r}\leq\lfloor nt\rfloor
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{i,ts}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(w_{n}-M)}\cdot\bigg\{\frac{1}{\lfloor nt\rfloor\lfloor ns\rfloor}{\bf\Omega}_{w_{n}-M,M}-\frac{1}{\lfloor nt\rfloor(n-\lfloor ns\rfloor)}{\bf\Omega}_{w_{n}-M,M}\bigg\}.

If
\lfloor ns\rfloor<[b_{i}^{l},b_{i}^{r}]<\lfloor nt\rfloor
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{i,ts}=-\frac{1}{(w_{n}-M)}\cdot\frac{1}{\lfloor nt\rfloor(n-\lfloor ns\rfloor)}{\bf\Omega}_{w_{n}-M,M}.

If
b_{i}^{l}\leq\lfloor nt\rfloor<b_{i}^{r}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{i,ts}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(w_{n}-M)}\cdot\bigg\{\frac{1}{(n-\lfloor nt\rfloor)(n-\lfloor ns\rfloor)}{\bf\Omega}_{w_{n}-M,M}-\frac{1}{(n-\lfloor nt\rfloor)\lfloor ns\rfloor}{\bf\Omega}_{w_{n}-M,M}\bigg\}.

If
b_{i}^{l}>\lfloor nt\rfloor
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{i,ts}=\frac{1}{(w_{n}-M)}\cdot\frac{1}{(n-\lfloor nt\rfloor)(n-\lfloor ns\rfloor)}{\bf\Omega}_{w_{n}-M,M}.

We consider the case: there exists
\ell_{n}^{{}^{\prime}}<\ell_{n}
, such that
b_{\ell_{n}^{{}^{\prime}}}^{r}\leq\lfloor ns\rfloor<b_{\ell_{n}^{{}^{\prime}}+1}^{l}
and
b_{\ell_{n}}^{r}\leq\lfloor nt\rfloor<b_{\ell_{n}+1}^{l}
